is the first vertex of
P^{\prime}
).

The size of the label is clearly
O(1)
.

To obtain
b_{1}
we simply check whether
\mathsf{first_{H}}(a,P)
(stored in item (
1a
) of the label of
a
appears before
f
on
P
. If so, since
P
has no outgoing edges,
\mathsf{first_{H}}(a,P)=\mathsf{first_{H\setminus f}}(a,P_{1})
. Otherwise,
P_{1}
is not reachable from
a
in
H\setminus f
.

Next we describe how to find
b_{2}=\mathsf{first_{\hat{H}^{\circ}\setminus Q}}(s,P_{2})
. If the vertex
u
stored in item (
2
) of the label of
f
falls inside one of the two intervals stored in item (
1b
) of the label of
a
then
b_{2}=u
.
Otherwise,
b_{2}
is the earliest starting endpoint that is later than
f
among the two endpoints of the two intervals stored in the label of
a
(if both intervals are before
f
on
P
then
b_{2}
does not exist).
∎

Figure 10:
When
f\in Q
: The vertical (blue) separator path
P
(in this example the separator
Q
consists of just a single path
P
) is partitioned by
f
into
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
. The vertex
z
is the first vertex of
p
’s (blue) path
P^{\prime}
. The brown paths are the ways that
z
can reach
P
in
\hat{H}^{\circ}\setminus Q
. The vertex
b
is the first vertex of
P_{2}
that is reachable from
p
in
\hat{H}^{\circ}\setminus Q
. In the left image,
f
lies inside the interval of vertices
P(p)
that are reachable from
p
(
b
is therefore stored in
f
). In the right image,
f
lies outside the two intervals
P(p)
(and
b
is therefore stored in
s
). The shaded area is the cycle
C
in the proof of Claim
4.8
.

4.4
The
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P\rightarrow P\setminus f}}
labeling (
Lemma

4.7
)

In this section we present the secondary labeling scheme (with polylogarithmic-size labels), which addresses the following problem: We are given a directed planar graph
G
and a single path
P
in
G
whose endpoints lie on the same face. We need to label the vertices of
P
such that given the labels of any two vertices
b,f
of
P
we can find the first vertex
p<f
of
P
that is reachable from
b
in
G\setminus\{f\}
and the first vertex
p>f
of
P
that is reachable from
b
in
G\setminus\{f\}
.

An auxiliary procedure

We begin with an auxiliary procedure that will be useful for our labeling.
In this procedure, we wish to label the vertices of
P
, such that given the labels of any two vertices
b,f
such that
f
is before
b
on
P
(i.e.,
f<b
), we can find the first vertex
p>f
of
P
that is reachable in
G
from
b
using a path that does not touch
f
or any vertex before
f
(i.e., does not touch any vertex
v\leq f
of
P
).

Let
H_{P}
be the graph composed of the path
P
and the following additional edges: for every pair of vertices
u,v\in P
where
u>v
, we add an edge
(u,v)
iff (1) there exists a
u
-to-
v
path in
G
that does not touch
P
before
v
, and (2) there is no such
w
-to-
v
path for any
w>u
. The following claim shows that instead of working with
G
, we can work with
H_{P}
:

Claim 4.9
.

Given any
f<b
, the first vertex
p>f
that is reachable from
b
using a path that does not touch
f
or any vertex of
P
before
f
, is the same in
G
and in
H_{P}
.

Proof.

Let
S
be the corresponding
b
-to-
p
path in
G
for
f<p<b
.
The path
S
visits no vertex of
P
before
f
(by definition of
S
) and no vertex of
P
between
f
and
p
(by definition of
p
). Hence, in
H_{P}
there is an edge
(u,p)
for some
u
where
u=b
or
u>b
, and so
S
is represented in
H_{P}
(by a path composed of a
b
-to-
u
prefix along
P
followed by the single edge
(u,p)
). In the other direction, let
R
be the corresponding
b
-to-
p
path in
H_{P}
. The path
R
visits no vertex of
P
before
f
(by definition of
R
) and every edge
(u,v)
of
R
corresponds to a path in
G
that visits no vertex of
P
before
v
(and therefore visits no vertex of
P
before
f
), hence
R
is appropriately represented in
G
.
∎

We call the edges of
H_{P}
that are not edges of
P

detours
.
We will use the fact that detours do not cross (i.e., they form a laminar family):

Claim 4.10
.

If there is a detour
(u,v)
then there is no detour
(w,x)
with
v<x<u<w
.

Proof.

By concatenating the
(w,x)
detour, the
x
-to-
u
subpath of
P
, and the
(u,v)
detour, we get a path in
G
that does not touch
P
before
v
but starts at a vertex
w>u
contradicting condition (2) of the
H_{P}
edges.
∎

We now explain what needs to be stored to facilitate the auxiliary procedure.
We define the
size

|d|
of a detour
d=(u,v)
as the number of vertices of
P
between
v
and
u
.
We say that a vertex of
P
is
contained
in a detour
d=(u,v)
if it lies on
P
between
v
and
u
. We say that a detour
d^{\prime}
is
contained
in detour
d
if all vertices that are contained in
d^{\prime}
are also contained in
d
.
Notice that, from Claim
4.9
, the vertex
p
sought by the auxiliary procedure is an endpoint of the largest detour
d
that contains
b
and does not contain
f
. To find
d
,
every vertex
v
of
P
stores a set of
O(\log n)
nested detours
d^{v}_{1},d^{v}_{2},\dots
where
d^{v}_{1}
is the largest detour containing
v
, and
d^{v}_{i+1}
is the largest detour of size
|d^{v}_{i+1}|\leq|d^{v}_{i}|/2
containing
v
. By Claim
4.10
, this set of nested detours is well defined.

Additionally, for each
d^{v}_{i}
,
v
stores the largest detour
\hat{d}{{}^{v}_{i}}
that is strictly contained in
d^{v}_{i}
but does not contain
d^{v}_{i+1}
.

See Figure
11
.

Figure 11:
A nested system of
O(\log n)
detours
d^{f}_{1},d^{f}_{2},\dots
of
f
(in red) used in the auxiliary procedure. The (green) detour
d
is the largest detour that contains
b
and does not contain
f
. Either
d=\hat{d}{{}^{f}_{j}}
and is stored in
f
, or
d=d^{b}_{i+1}
and is stored in
b
.

Consider the smallest detour
d^{f}_{j}=d^{b}_{i}
that is saved by both
b
and
f
. If such a detour does not exist then there is no detour that contains both
b
and
f
and so
d=d^{b}_{1}
is stored in
b
. If
|d|>|d^{f}_{j}|/2
then
d
must be the largest detour that is contained in
d^{f}_{j}
but does not contain
d^{f}_{j+1}
. In other words,
d=\hat{d}{{}^{f}_{j}}
and is stored in
f
. If on the other hand
|d|\leq|d^{f}_{j}|/2
, then
|d|\leq|d^{b}_{i}|/2
and by the choice of
d^{b}_{i}
we have that
d^{b}_{i+1}
does not contain
f
and so
d=d^{b}_{i+1}
and is stored in
b
. This completes the description of the auxiliary procedure.

Description of the
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P\rightarrow P\setminus f}}
labeling

Equipped with the above auxiliary procedure, we are now ready to describe the secondary labeling scheme. Recall that there are four cases to consider: Given
b,f\in P
where
b
can be before/after
f
we wish to find the first vertex
p
before/after
f
that is reachable from
b
in
G\setminus\{f\}
. There are four cases to consider:

Given
f<b
, find the first vertex
p<f
that is reachable from
b
in
G\setminus\{f\}
.
Consider the
b
-to-
p
path
R
in
G\setminus\{f\}
. Let
r
be the first vertex of
R
that belongs to
P
and
r<f
. Let
r^{\prime}
be the vertex of
P
that precedes
r
on
R
. Note that
r<f
, that
r^{\prime}>f
, and that the
r^{\prime}
-to-
r
subpath of
R
(which we call a
bypass
of
f
and denote
R[r^{\prime},r]
) is internally disjoint from
P
and it either emanates to the left or to the right of
P
. Moreover, since the endpoints of
P
lie on the same face, then if
R
emanates at
r^{\prime}
to the left (resp. right) of
P
it must enter
P
at
r
from the left (resp. right) of
P
. This means that we can assume w.l.o.g that the bypass
R[r^{\prime},r]
is the largest such bypass (in terms of the number of vertices of
P
between
r
and
r^{\prime}
). This is because any smaller bypass that is on the same side of
P
as
R[r^{\prime},r]
is either contained in
R[r^{\prime},r]
(in which case we might as well use
R[r^{\prime},r]
) or intersects
R[r^{\prime},r]
implying (in contradiction) that there is a larger bypass. See Figure
12
.

Figure 12:
A bypass
R[r^{\prime},r]
(in black) of
f
.
If there was an intersecting bypass (in green) that is larger than
R[r^{\prime},r]
then the green subpath before
x
and the black subpath after
x
would constitute a larger bypass.

We let
each vertex
f\in P
store the endpoints of the largest bypass
L_{f}
(resp.
R_{f}
) that is internally disjoint from
P
, emanates left (resp. right) of
P
at a vertex that is after
f
, and enters
P
from the left (resp. right) at a vertex that is before
f
. The vertex
f\in P
also stores the first vertex
L_{f}^{+}
(resp.
R_{f}^{+}
) of
P
that is before
f
and is reachable in
G\setminus\{f\}
from the endpoint of
L_{f}
(resp.
R_{f}
) that is after
f
.

In order to find
p
, we first use the auxiliary procedure of Section
4.4
to find the first vertex
p^{\prime}>f
that is reachable in
G
from
b
using a path that does not touch any vertex of
P
before
f
. Then, we check whether
p^{\prime}
is contained in
L_{f}
and if so we consider
L_{f}^{+}
as a candidate for
p
. Similarly, we check whether
p^{\prime}
is contained in
R_{f}
and if so we consider
R_{f}^{+}
as a candidate for
p
. Finally, we return the earlier of the (at most two) candidates.

Given
f<b
, find the first vertex
p>f
that is reachable from
b
in
G\setminus\{f\}
.

This case is very similar to the previous case. The only differences are: (1) we let
every vertex
f\in P
store the first vertex
L_{f}^{-}
(resp.
R_{f}^{-}
) of
P
that is after
f
and is reachable in
G\setminus\{f\}
from the endpoints of
L_{f}
(resp.
R_{f}
)
, and (2) we add
p^{\prime}
itself as a third possible candidate for
p
.

Given
b<f
, find the first vertex
p>f
that is reachable from
b
in
G\setminus\{f\}
.

This case and the next one are handled by small (but not symmetric) modifications of the previous two cases.
Consider the
b
-to-
p
path
R
in
G\setminus\{f\}
. Let
r
be the first vertex of
R
that belongs to
P
and
r>f
. Let
r^{\prime}
be the vertex of
P
that precedes
r
on
R
. The subpath
R[r^{\prime},r]
(which we call a
byway
of
f
) is internally disjoint from
P
, and if it emanates at
r^{\prime}
to the left (resp. right) of
P
then it must enter
r
from the left (resp. right) of
P
. We can assume w.l.o.g that
R[r^{\prime},r]
is the smallest such byway (because any larger byway that is on the same side of
P
as
R[r^{\prime},r]
either contains
R[r^{\prime},r]
(in which case we might as well use
R[r^{\prime},r]
) or intersects
R[r^{\prime},r]
implying (in contradiction) that there is a smaller byway. See Figure
13
.

We let
each vertex
f\in P
store the endpoints of the smallest byway
L_{f}
(resp.
R_{f}
) containing
f
that is to the left (resp. right) of
P
. The vertex
f\in P
also stores the first vertex
L_{f}^{-}
(resp.
R_{f}^{-}
) of
P
that is after
f
and is reachable in
G\setminus\{f\}
from the endpoint of
L_{f}
(resp.
R_{f}
) that is after
f
.

In order to find
p
, we begin by finding the first vertex
p^{\prime}<f
that is reachable in
G
from
b
using a path that does not touch any vertex of
P
after
f
. This is done by using a variant of the auxiliary procedure of Section
4.4
with the following modification. In
H_{P}
we add the detour
(u,v)
iff (1) there exists a
u
-to-
v
path in
G
that does not touch
P
before
v
, and (2) there is no such
u
-to-
w
path for any
w<v
.
After finding
p^{\prime}
using this mechanism, we check whether
p^{\prime}
appears on
P
before the starting point of the byway
L_{f}
, and if so we consider
L_{f}^{-}
as a candidate for
p
. Similarly, we check whether
p^{\prime}
appears on
P
before the starting point of the byway
R_{f}
and if so we consider
R_{f}^{-}
as a candidate for
p
. Finally, we return the earlier of the (at most two) candidates.

Figure 13:
Two byways of
f
(in black), the top one is
R[r^{\prime},r]
. If there was an intersecting byway (in green) that is smaller than
R[r^{\prime},r]
then the green subpath before
x
and the black subpath after
x
would constitute a smaller byway.

Given
b<f
, find the first vertex
p<f
that is reachable from
b
in
G\setminus\{f\}
.

This case is very similar to the previous case. The only differences are: (1) we
let every vertex
f\in P
store the first vertex
L_{f}^{+}
(resp.
R_{f}^{+}
) of
P
that is before
f
and is reachable in
G\setminus\{f\}
from the endpoints of
L_{f}
(resp.
R_{f}
),
and (2) we add
p^{\prime}
itself as a third possible candidate for
p
.

5
Approximate Distance Labeling

In the remainder of this paper, we extend our labeling scheme from reachability to approximate distances.
We use standard notation for weighted graphs.
For a path
P
we use
\mathsf{len}(P)
to denote the total length of
P
, i.e. the sum of
P
’s edge weights.
We also use
\mathsf{dist}_{H}(x,y)
to denote the
x
-to
y
distance in the graph
H
.

We begin by describing the scaling approach of Thorup
[
Tho04
]
.
A
(3,r)
-layered spanning tree
T
in a digraph
H
is an unoriented rooted spanning tree (i.e., a rooted spanning tree when ignoring the directions of edges) such that any path in
T
from the root is the concatenation of at most
3
shortest directed paths in
H
, each of length at most
r
.
We say that
H
is a
(3,r)
-layered graph if it has such a spanning tree.

Lemma 5.1
.

[
Tho04
, Lemma 3.2
7
7

7

The statement of item (3) in
[
Tho04
]
only concerns shortest paths, but the proof there actually applies to any path of length at most
r
, as stated here.
]

Given a directed graph
G
and a scale
r
, we can construct in linear time a series of directed graphs
G_{1}^{r},\dots,G_{k}^{r}
, such that:

1.

The total number of edges and vertices in all
G_{i}^{r}
’s is linear in the number of edges and vertices of
G
.

2.

Each vertex
v
of
G
has an index
i(v)
, such that
v
does not belong to any
G_{i}^{r}
other than
G_{i(v)}^{r}

G_{i(v)-1}^{r}
, and
G_{i(v)-2}^{r}
. Moreover, for any path
P
of length at most
r
that starts at
v
,
P
is a path in
G
if and only if
P
is a path in at least one of the three
G_{i}^{r}
’s containing
v
.

3.

Each
G_{i}^{r}
is a
(3,r)
-layered graph.

4.

G_{i}
is a minor of
G
. That is,
G_{i}
is obtained from
G
by deletion of edges and vertices, and contraction of edges.

We invoke
Lemma

5.1
at exponentially growing scales
r
(i.e., all powers of
2
up to
2nM
where
M
is the largest edge-weight in the graph).
To each of the resulting graphs
G_{i}^{r}
, we will apply a labeling scheme for reporting the length of an
s
-to-
t
path in
G\setminus\{f\}
that is with additive error at most
\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\cdot r
with respect to the length of the shortest such path.
By item (2) of the Lemma, the distance reported for one of the three graphs containing
s
at the scale
r
satisfying
2\mathsf{dist}_{G\setminus\{f\}}(s,t)\geq r\geq\mathsf{dist}_{G\setminus\{f\}}(s,t)
, is a
(1+\varepsilon)
multiplicative approximation of the correct answer, and no distance reported for any graph containing
s
will be smaller than the correct answer.

Hence, from now on we focus on describing the labeling scheme with additive error
\varepsilon r
for a
(3,r)
-layered directed planar graph.
The distance labeling scheme generalizes the reachability labeling scheme, but not in a black box manner.
We shall follow the overall structure of the reachability labels. In particular, the graph will be recursively decomposed using fundamental cycle separators, but now we use the the
(3,r)
-layered spanning tree
T
guaranteed by item (3) in
Lemma

5.1
.
Each separator
Q
still consists of a constant number of directed paths, but now this constant is larger (the spanning tree we use now is
3
-layered rather than
2
-layered for reachability), and now these paths are shortest paths, each of length at most
r
.
We further break each of these paths into
O(1/\varepsilon)
subpaths, each of length at most
\varepsilon r
. Hence, from now on we treat every separator
Q
as a set of
O(1/\varepsilon)
directed shortest paths of length
\varepsilon r
each.

To explain the high level idea of our approach, let us describe how to generalize from reachability to approximate distances in the
non-faulty
case.
Assume we just want to approximate the length of a shortest path from
s
to
t
under the assumption that this shortest path intersects a particular separator path
P
.
In the case of reachability, we could deduce the answer by comparing
\mathsf{first_{G}}(s,P)
and
\mathsf{last_{G}}(P,t)
.
To generalize to approximate distances, let
\mathsf{first^{\alpha}_{G}}(s,P)
(resp.,
\mathsf{last^{\alpha}_{G}}(t,P)
) denote the first (resp., last) vertex of
P
that is reachable from
s
(resp., can reach
t
) in
G
via a path of length at most
\alpha
. We store
\mathsf{first^{i\varepsilon r}_{G}}(s,P)
and
\mathsf{last^{i\varepsilon r}_{G}}(t,P)
for every integer
0\leq i\leq 1/\varepsilon
.
To answer the query, we find
i,j
minimizing
(i+j)
such that
\mathsf{first^{i\varepsilon r}_{G}}(s,P)\leq_{P}\mathsf{last^{j\varepsilon r}_{G}}(t,P)
, and return
(i+j+1)\varepsilon r
as the estimated distance.

This is correct since
\mathsf{first^{i\varepsilon r}_{G}}(s,P)\leq_{P}\mathsf{last^{j\varepsilon r}_{G}}(t,P)
implies there exists an
s
-to-
t
path of length at most
(i+j+1)\varepsilon r
(the
+1
term is for going along the path
P
whose length is at most
\varepsilon r
).
To see the approximation guarantee, consider the shortest
s
-to-
t
path
\Gamma
that intersects
P
. Let
\alpha
be the length of the prefix of
\Gamma
ending at the earliest vertex of
P
visited by
\Gamma
. Let
\beta
be the length of the maximal suffix of
\Gamma
that is internally disjoint from
P
. Then, for
i=\lceil\frac{\alpha}{\varepsilon r}\rceil
, and
j=\lceil\frac{\beta}{\varepsilon r}\rceil
,
\mathsf{first^{i\varepsilon r}_{G}}(s,P)\leq_{P}\mathsf{last^{j\varepsilon r}_{G}}(t,P)
, so we will return at most
(i+j+1)\varepsilon r\leq\alpha+\beta+3\varepsilon r
, while the length of
\Gamma
is at least
\alpha+\beta
.

We note that Thorup’s distance labels use the idea of
portals
(a.k.a
\varepsilon
-covers) which results in smaller labels and more efficient query algorithm.
Our more brute force approach incurs polynomial factors in
1/\varepsilon
, but allows us to use the ideas of the fault-tolerant reachability mechanism from the previous sections.

Note, however, that our scheme for fault-tolerant reachability requires in some cases to break the
s
-to-
t
path into more than two segments (yet still a constant number of segments), and requires additional modifications as we explain in the rest of this section. In the following sections, some of the text is a straightforward adaptation of the corresponding text for reachability implementing the high-level ideas above. However, in several places (which we will highlight) significant changes and additional ideas are required.

5.1
Fault tolerant approximate distance labeling

In this section, we describe our approximate distances labeling scheme. I.e., what to store in the labels so that given the labels of any three vertices
s,f,t
we can approximate
\mathsf{dist}_{G\setminus\{f\}}(s,t)
.
We follow the structure of the reachability labels from
Section

4
, and adjust it to the more complicated task of approximate distances. Our main result is the following theorem.

Theorem 5.2
.

There exists a labeling scheme for a planar graph
G
that, given vertices
s,t,f
returns a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation of
\mathsf{dist}_{G\setminus\{f\}}(s,t)
.
The size of each label is
\tilde{O}(\mathsf{poly}(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
.

Before defining the analogous of
Lemmas

4.3
and
4.2
from
Section

4
, we introduce the analog of
\mathsf{first_{F}}(v,P)
.
For a graph
F
, a vertex
v
, a path
P
, and a number
\alpha
, let
\mathsf{first^{\alpha}_{F}}(v,P)
denote the first vertex on
P
that is reachable from
v
in
F
via a path of length at most
\alpha
.
We also present the following relaxation of
\mathsf{first^{\alpha}_{F}}(u,P)
.

Definition 5.3

(
\delta
-
\mathsf{first}
)
.

Let
F
be a directed graph with edge weights from
\mathbb{R}^{+}
, and let
P
be a path.
A vertex
v\in F
is
\mathsf{\delta\text{-}first^{\alpha}_{F}}(u,P)
if

1.

v\leq_{P}\mathsf{first^{\alpha}_{F}}(u,P)
and,

2.

\mathsf{dist}_{F}(u,v)\leq\alpha+\delta
.

We follow the reduction into two labeling schemes from a vertex to a path in
the presence of a failed vertex, one for the case that the failing vertex is not on the path, and the
other for the case that it is.

Lemma 5.4
.

There exists a labeling scheme
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{s\xrightarrow{f}P}}=\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{s\xrightarrow{f}P}}_{G,P,r,\alpha,\varepsilon}
where
G
is a planar graph equipped with a decomposition tree
\mathcal{T}
,
P
is a path in
\mathcal{T}
with
\mathsf{len}(P)=0
, and
r,\alpha,\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
such that
\alpha\leq r
, and the length of every separator in
\mathcal{T}
is bounded by
r
.
Let
s
and
f\notin P
be two vertices of
G
that are not an ancestor apex of one another, and such that
P
is an ancestor path of both
s
and
f
.
Given the labels of
s
and
f
, one can compute the index on
P
of some vertex
b
that is
\mathsf{\varepsilon r\text{-}first^{\alpha}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(s,P)
.
In this labeling scheme, the only vertices that store a label are those that have
P
as an ancestor. The size of each label stored by such a vertex is
\tilde{O}(\mathsf{poly}(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
.

Lemma 5.5
.

There exists a labeling scheme
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{s\xrightarrow{}P\setminus\{f\}}}=\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{s\xrightarrow{}P\setminus\{f\}}}_{G,P,r,\alpha,\varepsilon}
where
G
is a planar graph equipped with a decomposition tree
\mathcal{T}
, and
P
is a path in
\mathcal{T}
such that both endpoints of
P
lie on the same face of
G
and
\mathsf{len}(P)=0
and
r,\alpha,\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
such that
\alpha\leq r
, and the length of every separator in
\mathcal{T}
is bounded by
r
.
Let
s
and
f\in P
be two vertices of
G
that are not an ancestor apex of one another, and such that
P
is an ancestor path of both
s
and
f
.
Given the labels of
s
and
f
, one can compute the indices on
P
of some vertices
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
that are
\mathsf{\varepsilon r\text{-}first^{\alpha}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(s,P_{1})
and
\mathsf{\varepsilon r\text{-}first^{\alpha}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(s,P_{2})
, respectively, where
P_{1}
(resp.
P_{2}
) is the prefix (resp. suffix) of
P
that precedes (resp. follows)
f
, excluding
f
.
In this labeling scheme, the only vertices that store a label are those that have
P
as an ancestor path. The size of each label stored by such a vertex is
\tilde{O}(\mathsf{poly}(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
.

We prove the above two lemmas in
Sections

5.2
and
5.3
.
Given these lemmas, we now prove our main theorem.

Proof of
Theorem

5.2
.

Let
G_{rev}
be the graph obtained from
G
by reversing all edges (
G_{rev}
has exactly the same decomposition tree as
G
, but the direction of each path is reversed).
Let
\varepsilon^{\prime}=\frac{\varepsilon}{10}
be an approximation parameter, and for every
r\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
let
\Gamma_{r}=\{i\varepsilon^{\prime}r\mid i\in[\left\lceil{\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{\prime}}}\right\rceil]\}
.

Let
v
be a vertex.
For each
r
that is a power of
2
between
1
and
2nM
, for every
i
such that
v\in G^{r}_{i}
, and for every
\alpha,\beta\in\Gamma_{r}
the label of
v
stores the following:

1.

For each piece
H
in the recursive decomposition
\mathcal{T}
of
G^{r}_{i}
such that
v\in H\setminus\partial H
,
v
stores its label in the standard (non-faulty) approximate distance labeling of Thorup for
H\setminus\partial H
.

2.

For every ancestor piece
H
in
G^{r}_{i}
,
v
stores
\mathsf{first^{\beta}_{H}}(v,P)
for each of the
\tilde{O}(\mathsf{poly}(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
paths
P
of
\partial H
.

3.

Using
Lemma

5.4
,
for every ancestor path
P
of
v
in the recursive decomposition
\mathcal{T}
,
v
stores
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{s\xrightarrow{f}P}}_{G^{\prime},P,r,\alpha,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(v)
where
G^{\prime}
is obtained from
G^{r}_{i}
by setting
\mathsf{len}(P)=0
.

4.

Using
Lemma

5.5
,
for every ancestor piece
H
of
v
in
\mathcal{T}
, for every path
P
of the separator
C
of
H
,
v
stores
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{s\xrightarrow{}P\setminus\{f\}}}_{H^{\times P},P,r,\alpha,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(v)
, where
H^{\times P}
is the graph obtained from
H\setminus\partial H
by making an incision along all the edges of
C
other than those of
P
and setting
\mathsf{len}(P)=0
.
Note that, because of the incision, the endpoints of
P
lie on a single face of
H^{\times P}
, so
Lemma

5.5
indeed applies.

5.

For every ancestor apex
a
of
v
, for every ancestor path
P
of
v
,
v
stores in its label
\mathsf{first^{\alpha}_{G^{r}_{i}\setminus\{a\}}}(v,P)
, and
\mathsf{first^{\alpha}_{G^{r}_{i}\setminus\{v\}}}(a,P)
.

If
a
lies on
P
then let
P_{2}
be the suffix of
P
after
a
(not including
f
).

v
also stores
\mathsf{first^{\alpha}_{G^{r}_{i}\setminus\{a\}}}(v,P_{2})
.
Similarly, if
v
lies on
P
then let
P_{2}
be the suffix of
P
after
v
(not including
v
).

v
also stores
\mathsf{first^{\alpha}_{G^{r}_{i}\setminus\{v\}}}(a,P_{2})
.

6.

v
additionally stores all the above items in the graph
(G^{r}_{i})_{rev}
instead of the graph
G^{r}_{i}
.

Size.

Each vertex participates in
O(\log Mn)
graphs
G^{r}_{i}
, and
|\Gamma_{r}|=O(1/\varepsilon)
.
Thus, for each
G^{r}_{i}
such that
v\in G^{r}_{i}
and for every
\alpha,\beta\in\Gamma_{r}
,
v
has only
\tilde{O}(\mathsf{poly}(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
ancestor pieces, paths and apices, and by
Lemmas

5.4
and
5.5
, all items above sum up to a label of size
\tilde{O}(\mathsf{poly}(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
.

Decoding and Correctness.

Let
R
be a shortest path from
s
to
t
in
G\setminus\{f\}
.
By the second property of
Lemma

5.1
, there exists a graph
G^{r}_{i}
such that
R
is contained in
G^{r}_{i}
.
If
f\notin G^{r}_{i}
we query Thorup’s non-faulty approximate distances labels for
G^{r}_{i}
(stored in item (
1
)).
This will output a
(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})
-approximation of
\mathsf{dist}_{G^{r}_{i}}(s,t)=\mathsf{dist}_{G\setminus\{f\}}(s,t)
.
Otherwise, let
\hat{H}
be the rootmost piece in
\mathcal{T}
whose separator
Q
separates
t
and
f
.
Let
H
be a child piece of
\hat{H}
that contains
t
(if both children of
\hat{H}
contain
t
then, if one of the children does not contain
f
we choose
H
to be that child).
Note that by choice of
H
,
f\notin H\setminus\partial H
.

We assume without loss of generality that
s\in\hat{H}
. We handle the other case symmetrically to the description below, by swapping the roles of
s
and
t
and working in
(G^{r}_{i})_{rev}
instead of in
G^{r}_{i}
.

Observe that by definition of
H
and of separation,
f\in\partial H
iff
f\in Q
.
Consider first the case when
R
does not touch
\partial H
. i.e.,
s,t
and
R
are all contained in
H\setminus\partial H
, and
f
is not contained in
H\setminus\partial H
. In this case, querying Thorup’s non-faulty labels for
H\setminus\partial H
(stored in item (
1
)) will output a
(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})
-approximation of
\mathsf{dist}_{H\setminus\partial H}(s,t)=\mathsf{dist}_{G\setminus\{f\}}(s,t)
.

To treat the case where the replacement path
R
touches
\partial H
, we separately handle the cases where
f\notin Q
and
f\in Q
.

When
f\notin Q
(and so,
f\notin\partial H
).

In this case,
R
must have a suffix contained in
H
, and this suffix is unaffected by the fault
f
.
Let
a
be the last vertex on
R
which is on
Q
, and let
\alpha
and
\beta
be the smallest numbers in
\Gamma_{r}
with
\alpha\geq\mathsf{len}(R[s,a])
and
\beta\geq\mathsf{len}(R[a,t])
.
Let
P
be the subpath of
Q
that contains
a
.
If
R
exists then
\mathsf{first^{\alpha}_{G^{r}_{i}\setminus\{f\}}}(s,P)<_{P}\mathsf{first^{\beta}_{H_{rev}}}(t,P)
.
The vertex
\mathsf{first^{\beta}_{H_{rev}}}(t,P)
is stored in item (
2
) of the label of
t
in
(G^{r}_{i})_{rev}
.
Notice that by the rootmost choice of
\hat{H}
,
H
is an ancestor piece of
t
, so
t
indeed stores
\mathsf{first^{\beta}_{H_{rev}}}(t,P)
.
It thus remains only to describe how to find
\mathsf{first^{\alpha}_{G^{r}_{i}\setminus\{f\}}}(s,P)
from the labels of
s
and
f
.
If either
s
or
f
store
\mathsf{first^{\alpha}_{G^{r}_{i}\setminus\{f\}}}(s,P)
in item (
5
), we are done.
Otherwise neither
s
nor
f
is an ancestor apex of the other, and since both
s
and
f
are in
\hat{H}
,
P
is indeed an ancestor path of both
s
and
f
, so, by
Lemma

5.4
, a vertex
a^{\prime}
that is an
\mathsf{\varepsilon^{\prime}r\text{-}first^{\alpha}_{G^{r}_{i}\setminus\{f\}}}(s,P)
can be obtained from
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{s\xrightarrow{f}P}}_{G^{\prime},P,r,\alpha,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(s)
(stored in item (
3
) of the label of
s
) and
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{s\xrightarrow{f}P}}_{G^{\prime},P,r,\alpha,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(f)
(stored in item (
3
) of the label of
f
).
The decoding algorithm will recognize that indeed
a^{\prime}\leq_{P}\mathsf{first^{\beta}_{H_{rev}}}(t,P)
and deduce that there exists a path of length at most
(\alpha+\varepsilon^{\prime}r)+\varepsilon^{\prime}r+\beta
from
s
to
t
in
G\setminus\{f\}
.
Thus, the distance we output is

\displaystyle(\alpha+\varepsilon^{\prime}r)+\varepsilon^{\prime}r+\beta

\displaystyle\leq(\alpha+\beta)+2\varepsilon^{\prime}r

\displaystyle\leq|R|+4\varepsilon^{\prime}r

\displaystyle\leq|R|+8\varepsilon^{\prime}|R|

\displaystyle\leq(1+\varepsilon)|R|=(1+\varepsilon)\mathsf{dist}_{G\setminus\{f\}}(s,t).

When
f\in Q
.

Let
P
be the path of
Q
that contains
f
.
Consider first the case where the path
R
touches some path
\hat{P}\neq P
of
Q
or of the boundary of some ancestor of
H
. Since boundary paths are vertex disjoint,
f\in P
implies
f\notin\hat{P}
.
Hence, we can obtain a vertex
a^{\prime}
that is an
\mathsf{\varepsilon^{\prime}r\text{-}first^{\alpha}_{G^{r}_{i}\setminus\{f\}}}(s,\hat{P})
in a similar manner to the case
f\notin Q
above, with
\hat{P}
taking the role of
P
.
In an analogous manner, we can obtain a vertex
b^{\prime}
that is a
\mathsf{\varepsilon^{\prime}r\text{-}first^{\alpha}_{(G^{r}_{i})_{rev}\setminus\{f\}}}(t,\hat{P})
as we just found
a^{\prime}
, but with
\hat{P}
taking the role of
P
,
t
taking the role of
s
, and
(G^{r}_{i})_{rev}
taking the role of
G^{r}_{i}
(we cannot use part (
2
) of the label of
s
or
t
in this case because now
f
does belong to
\partial H
).

Now consider the case where other than
P
,
R
does not touch any path of
Q
or any path of the boundary of an ancestor of
H
. In this case, it is valid to use labels in
\hat{H}^{\times P}
instead of in
G^{r}_{i}
because
R
does touch
\partial\hat{H}
, and only crosses
Q
at
P
.
Let
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
be the prefix and suffix obtained from
P
by deleting
f
.
If either
s
or
f
is an ancestor apex of one another then

\mathsf{first^{\alpha}_{G^{r}_{i}\setminus\{f\}}}(s,P_{2})
is stored in item (
5
) of either
s
or
t
, and, if
\mathsf{first^{\alpha}_{G^{r}_{i}\setminus\{f\}}}(s,P_{1})
exists, then it is equal to
\mathsf{first^{\alpha}_{G^{r}_{i}\setminus\{f\}}}(s,P)
, which is also stored in item (
5
) of either
s
or
t
.
(If
\mathsf{first^{\alpha}_{G^{r}_{i}\setminus\{f\}}}(s,P)
is not a vertex of
P_{1}
then
\mathsf{first^{\alpha}_{G^{r}_{i}\setminus\{f\}}}(s,P_{1})
does not exist.)
If neither
s
nor
t
is an ancestor apex of the other, then
P
is an ancestor path of both
s,t
and
f
.
Let
a
be the last vertex on
R
which is on
P
, and let
\alpha
and
\beta
be the smallest numbers in
\Gamma_{r}
where
\alpha\geq\mathsf{len}(R[s,a])
and
\beta\geq\mathsf{len}(R[a,t])
.
We use the labels
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{s\xrightarrow{}P\setminus\{f\}}}_{\hat{H}^{\times P},P,r,\alpha,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(s),\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{s\xrightarrow{}P\setminus\{f\}}}_{\hat{H}^{\times P},P,r,\alpha,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(f),\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{s\xrightarrow{}P\setminus\{f\}}}_{(\hat{H}^{\times P})_{rev},P,r,\beta,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(f)
and
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{s\xrightarrow{}P\setminus\{f\}}}_{(\hat{H}^{\times P})_{rev},P,r,\beta,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(t)
to obtain the following vertices:

1.

a_{1}
which is an
\mathsf{\varepsilon^{\prime}r\text{-}first^{\alpha}_{\hat{H}^{\times P}}}(s,P_{1})

2.

a_{2}
which is an
\mathsf{\varepsilon^{\prime}r\text{-}first^{\alpha}_{\hat{H}^{\times P}}}(s,P_{2})
,

3.

b_{1}
which is an
\mathsf{\varepsilon^{\prime}r\text{-}first^{\beta}_{(\hat{H}^{\times P})_{rev}}}(s,P_{1})

4.

b_{2}
which is an
\mathsf{\varepsilon^{\prime}r\text{-}first^{\beta}_{(\hat{H}^{\times P})_{rev}}}(s,P_{2})

The decoding algorithm will recognize that indeed we have
a_{1}\leq_{P}b_{1}
(if
a\in P_{1}
) or
a_{2}\leq_{P}b_{2}
(if
a\in P_{2}
) and deduce that there exists a path of length at most
(\alpha+\varepsilon^{\prime}r)+\varepsilon^{\prime}r+(\beta+\varepsilon^{\prime}r)
from
s
to
t
in
G\setminus\{f\}
.
Thus, the distance we output is

\displaystyle(\alpha+\varepsilon^{\prime}r)+\varepsilon^{\prime}r+(\beta+\varepsilon^{\prime}r)

\displaystyle\leq(\alpha+\beta)+3\varepsilon^{\prime}r

\displaystyle\leq|R|+5\varepsilon^{\prime}r

\displaystyle\leq|R|+10\varepsilon^{\prime}|R|

\displaystyle\leq(1+\varepsilon)|R|=(1+\varepsilon)\mathsf{dist}_{G\setminus\{f\}}(s,t).

Notice that the decoding algorithm does not know a-priori the correct values of
G^{r}_{i}
,
\alpha
and
\beta
.
Thus, the algorithm iterates over all possible values and returns the minimum distance found.
Clearly, every distance found for some values implies a path in
G\setminus\{f\}
, thus the algorithm will always return at least
\mathsf{dist}_{G\setminus\{f\}}(s,t)
, and as we proved it will always be at most
(1+\varepsilon)\mathsf{dist}_{G\setminus\{f\}}(s,t)
.
∎

5.2
The
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{s\xrightarrow{f}P}}
labeling

In this section we provide a labeling scheme
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{s\xrightarrow{f}P}}
, proving
Lemma

5.4
.
Our labeling scheme makes use of the following two auxiliary labeling schemes:

Lemma 5.6
.

There exists a (trivial) labeling scheme
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{s\rightarrow P^{\prime}}}=\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{s\rightarrow P^{\prime}}}_{H,P,\alpha}
where
H
is a planar graph and
P
is a
0
-length path.
Given the label of a vertex
a
, one can retrieve the vertex
\mathsf{first^{\alpha}_{H}}(a,P)
.
The size of each label is
\tilde{O}(\mathsf{poly}(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
.

Lemma 5.7
.

There exists a labeling scheme
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P^{\prime}\xrightarrow{f}P}}=\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P^{\prime}\xrightarrow{f}P}}_{H,P,P^{\prime},\alpha,\varepsilon}
where
H
is a planar graph,
P
and
P^{\prime}
are two
0
-length paths and
\alpha,\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
.
Given the labels of a vertex
a
on
P^{\prime}
and a vertex
f
not in
P^{\prime}\cup P
, one can retrieve the index on
P
of some vertex
b\in P
such that
b
is an
\mathsf{\varepsilon\alpha\text{-}first^{\alpha}_{H\setminus f}}(a,P)
.
The size of each label is
\tilde{O}(\mathsf{poly}(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
.

The proof of
Lemma

5.6
is trivial and the proof of
Lemma

5.7
appears in
Section

7
.

Conceptually,
Lemma

5.4
is obtained by composing
Lemmas

5.7
and
5.6
, as we describe in the following overview.
However, the concept of composing two label schemes introduces several technical details, making the proof presented below appear more intricate.

Figure 14:
An lustration of a path from
s
to
b=\mathsf{first^{\alpha}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(s,P)
.
The blue subpath is from
s
to
a\in P^{\prime}
, and the gray subpath is from
a
to
b\in P
.
We use
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{s\rightarrow P^{\prime}}}
to find a good middle point for
a
on
P^{\prime}
, and
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P^{\prime}\xrightarrow{f}P}}
to find a good middle point for
b
on
P
.

Overview (see
Figure

14
).

Let
R
be a shortest path from
s
to
b=\mathsf{first^{\alpha}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(s,P)
in
G\setminus\{f\}
, and let
Q
be a separator that separates
s
and
f
.
We are interested in a special vertex on
R
: the first vertex
a^{*}
on
R
that is in
Q
, and specifically on some
P^{\prime}
which is an
\varepsilon r
-subpath of
Q
.
We ’guess’ estimations
\beta,\gamma
for the lengths of
R[s,a^{*}]
and
R[a^{*},b]
, respectively.
In this overview, it is useful to consider a vertex
m
that is
\mathsf{\varepsilon r\text{-}first^{\beta}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(s,P)
as a sufficiently good ’middle point.
Specifically, if one aims to reach an early vertex on
P
from
s
within a budget
\alpha
, requiring the path to pass through
m
and allowing the budget to exceed
\alpha
by
\varepsilon r
does not worsen the result on
P
.
Using the labels
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{s\rightarrow P^{\prime}}}
with budget
\beta
, we find a sufficiently good ’middle point’
a
for replacing
a^{*}
.
Starting from
a
, we use the labels
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P^{\prime}\xrightarrow{f}P}}
with budget
\gamma
to find a vertex earlier on
P
than
\mathsf{first^{\gamma}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(a,P)\leq_{P}\mathsf{first^{\gamma}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(a^{*},P)
.

Since each middle point is reachable with a budget increase of
O(\varepsilon r)
, the final point is reachable with a budget increase of
O(\varepsilon r)
.
Since every middle point is ’reasonably good’, and the budget used in each phase is an estimation of the length of the corresponding subpath of
R
, it is guaranteed that the final destination is at least as good as
b
.

We are now ready to provide the formal proof of
Lemma

5.4
.

See
5.4

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon^{\prime}=\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\in\Theta(\varepsilon)
be an approximation parameter and let
\Gamma=\{i\varepsilon^{\prime}\alpha\mid i\in[\left\lceil{\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{\prime}}}\right\rceil]\}
.
For a subpath
P^{\prime}
of a separator in the fully recursive decomposition of
G
, we denote as
G^{0}_{P^{\prime}}
the graph obtained from
G
by setting the weight of every edge in
P^{\prime}
to be
0
.
For every
\gamma\in\Gamma
let
G^{1}_{P^{\prime},\gamma}
be the graph
G
where the label of each vertex
v
is set to be
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P^{\prime}\xrightarrow{f}P}}_{G^{0}_{P^{\prime}},P,P^{\prime},\gamma,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(v)
.
We also define
G^{2}_{P^{\prime},\gamma}
as the subgraph of
G^{1}_{P^{\prime},\gamma}
induced only on vertices below the separator of
P^{\prime}
in the fully recursive decomposition of
G
.

The Labeling.

For every vertex
v
such that
P
is an ancestor of
v
in
\mathcal{T}
, the label of
v
stores

for every triplet
(P^{\prime},\beta,\gamma)
such that
P^{\prime}
is an
\varepsilon^{\prime}r
-subpath ancestor of
v
and
\beta,\gamma\in\Gamma
, the label
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{s\rightarrow P^{\prime}}}_{G^{2}_{P^{\prime},\gamma},P^{\prime},\beta}(v)
and the label of
v
in
G^{1}_{P^{\prime},\gamma}
.
Moreover, the label of
v
stores the identifiers of the ancestor pieces of
v
in the full recursive decomposition of
G
.
Finally, for every ancestor separator
Q
of
v
below
P
, the label of
v
stores
b^{\beta}_{Q}=\mathsf{first^{\beta}_{G^{\prime}_{Q}}}(v,P)
where
G^{\prime}_{Q}
is the subgraph below the separator of
Q
, and the label
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P^{\prime}\xrightarrow{f}P}}_{G,P,P,\gamma,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(b^{\beta}_{Q})
.

8
8

8

Notice that
\mathsf{first^{\alpha}_{G^{\prime}_{Q}}}(v,P)
is a slight abuse of notation, since
P
is not contained in
G^{\prime}_{Q}
. Note that the original definition of this notation can be applied similarly even if
P
is only partially contained in the graph. In this context,
\mathsf{first^{\alpha}_{G^{\prime}_{Q}}}(v,P)
is the first vertex of
P
obtainable from
v
via a path in
G^{\prime}_{Q}
with length at most
\alpha

Size.

There are
O(\log n)
ancestor separators of
v
in the fully recursive decomposition.
Each separator is partitioned into
O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon})
subpaths, so overall there are
\tilde{O}(\mathsf{poly}(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
options for
P^{\prime}
for each vertex
v
.
It follows from
|\Gamma|=O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon})
that there are
\tilde{O}(\mathsf{poly}(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
triplets
(P^{\prime},\beta,\gamma)
.
For every such triplet, the label stores an
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{s\rightarrow P^{\prime}}}
-type label in
G^{2}_{P^{\prime},\gamma}
and the label of a vertex in
G^{2}_{P^{\prime},\gamma}
.
Each vertex in
G^{2}_{P^{\prime},\gamma}
is labeled with a
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P^{\prime}\xrightarrow{f}P}}
-type label, which is of size
\tilde{O}(\mathsf{poly}(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
due to
Lemma

5.7
.
Due to
Lemma

5.6
, the size of
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{s\rightarrow P^{\prime}}}
is
\tilde{O}(\mathsf{poly}(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
, which should be multiplied by another
\tilde{O}(\mathsf{poly}(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
factor due to vertices of
G_{P^{\prime},\gamma}
each having labels of size
\tilde{O}(\mathsf{poly}(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
.
Finally, the height of the recursive decomposition is logarithmic, so storing the identifiers of ancestor pieces of
v
and storing
b^{\beta}_{Q}
and a
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P^{\prime}\xrightarrow{f}P}}
-type label of
b^{\beta}_{Q}
per ancestor separator
Q
of
v
increases the size of the label by
\tilde{O}(\mathsf{poly}(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
.

Decoding.

Given the labels of two vertices
s
and
f
such that
P
is an ancestor of
s
and
f
, and none of
s
and
f
is an apex ancestor of the other in
\mathcal{T}
.
The algorithm finds an
\mathsf{\varepsilon r\text{-}first^{\alpha}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(s,P)
vertex as follows.
The algorithm starts by finding the highest separator
Q
in the recursive decomposition that has
s
in one side, and
f
strictly in the other side.
The label of
s
stores
b^{\beta}_{Q}=\mathsf{first^{\alpha}_{G^{\prime}_{Q}}}(s,P)
and
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P^{\prime}\xrightarrow{f}P}}_{G,P,P,\gamma,\varepsilon}(b^{\beta}_{Q})
for every
\beta,\gamma\in\Gamma
.
The label of
f
stores
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P^{\prime}\xrightarrow{f}P}}_{G,P,P,\gamma,\varepsilon}(f)
.
We use the two labels to obtain
b^{\beta,\gamma}_{Q}
that is a
\mathsf{\varepsilon^{\prime}\gamma\text{-}first^{\gamma}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(b^{\beta}_{Q},P)
.

Recall that
Q
is partitioned into
O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon})
subpaths with length at most
\varepsilon^{\prime}r
each.
For each such subpath
P^{\prime}
, and for every
\beta,\gamma\in\Gamma
such that
\beta+\gamma\leq(1+2\varepsilon^{\prime})\alpha
, the algorithm uses
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{s\rightarrow P^{\prime}}}_{G^{2}_{P^{\prime},\gamma},P^{\prime},\beta}(s)
to find
a_{P^{\prime},\beta}=\mathsf{first^{\beta}_{G^{\prime}_{Q}}}(s,P^{\prime})
where
G^{\prime}_{Q}
is the subgraph induced by vertices below
Q
in the fully recursive decomposition.
More precisely, the label
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{s\rightarrow P^{\prime}}}_{G^{2}_{P^{\prime},\gamma},P^{\prime},\beta}(s)
allows the algorithm to retrieve
\ell_{a}=\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P^{\prime}\xrightarrow{f}P}}_{G^{0}_{P^{\prime}},P,P^{\prime},\gamma,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(a_{P^{\prime},\beta})
.
Note that, the label of
f
contains
\ell_{f}=\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P^{\prime}\xrightarrow{f}P}}_{G^{0}_{P^{\prime}},P,P^{\prime},\gamma,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(f)
.
The algorithm uses the labels
\ell_{a}
and
\ell_{f}
to retrieve a vertex
b_{P^{\prime},\beta,\gamma}
on
P
that is an
\mathsf{\varepsilon^{\prime}\gamma\text{-}first^{\gamma}_{G^{0}_{P^{\prime}}\setminus\{f\}}}(a_{P^{\prime},\beta},P)
.
Finally, the algorithm returns the vertex
b=\min_{\leq P}\{b_{P^{\prime},\beta,\gamma},b^{\beta,\gamma}_{Q}\mid\beta,\gamma\in\Gamma,P^{\prime}\textit{ is a subpath of }Q,\beta+\gamma\leq(1+2\varepsilon^{\prime})\alpha\}
.
That is, the first vertex on
P
that was found over all choices of
P^{\prime}
,
\beta
and
\gamma
, or the first
b^{\beta,\gamma}_{Q}
found on
P
.

Correctness.

We show that when decoding the labels of two vertices
s
and
f
, we indeed return a vertex
b
that is an
\mathsf{\varepsilon r\text{-}first^{\alpha}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(s,P)
.
We start by showing that for every candidate
x
found by the decoding algorithm for
b
, we have
\mathsf{dist}_{G\setminus\{f\}}(s,x)\leq\alpha+\varepsilon r
.
Let
Q
be the lowest separator in the decomposition such that
s
is in one side of
Q
and
f
is strictly in the other side.
Considered a vertex
b^{\beta,\gamma}_{Q}
that is a candidate for being
b
returned by the decoding algorithm.
Recall that
b^{\beta,\gamma}_{Q}
is an
\mathsf{\varepsilon^{\prime}\gamma\text{-}first^{\gamma}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(b^{\beta}_{Q},P)
with
b^{\beta}_{Q}=\mathsf{first^{\beta}_{G^{\prime}_{Q}}}(s,P)
.
By definition, we have
\mathsf{dist}_{G^{\prime}_{Q}}(s,b^{\beta}_{Q})\leq\beta
and since
f\notin G^{\prime}_{Q}
we have
\mathsf{dist}_{G\setminus\{f\}}(s,b^{\beta}_{Q})\leq\beta
.
By definition,
\mathsf{dist}_{G\setminus\{f\}}(b^{\beta}_{Q},b^{\beta,\gamma}_{Q})\leq(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})\gamma
.
By triangle inequality we have
\mathsf{dist}_{G\setminus\{f\}}(s,b^{\beta,\gamma}_{Q})\leq\beta+\gamma+\varepsilon^{\prime}\gamma\leq\alpha+2\varepsilon^{\prime}\alpha+\varepsilon^{\prime}\alpha\leq(1+\varepsilon)\alpha
.

We now deal with candidates obtained as
b_{P^{\prime},\beta,\gamma}
.
Recall that
b_{P^{\prime},\beta,\gamma}
is an
\mathsf{\varepsilon^{\prime}\gamma\text{-}first^{\gamma}_{G^{0}_{P^{\prime}}\setminus\{f\}}}(a_{P^{\prime},\beta},P)
with
a_{P^{\prime},\beta}=\mathsf{first^{\beta}_{G^{\prime}_{Q}}}(s,P^{\prime})
.
Since
f\notin G^{\prime}_{Q}
we have
\mathsf{dist}_{G\setminus\{f\}}(s,a_{P^{\prime},\beta})\leq\mathsf{dist}_{G^{\prime}_{Q}}(s,a_{P^{\prime},\beta})\leq\beta
.
Since
b_{P^{\prime},\beta,\gamma}
is
\mathsf{\varepsilon^{\prime}\gamma\text{-}first^{\gamma^{*}}_{G^{0}_{P^{\prime}}\setminus\{f\}}}(a_{P^{\prime},\beta},P)
, we have
\mathsf{dist}_{G^{0}_{P^{\prime}}\setminus\{f\}}(a_{P^{\prime},\beta},b_{P^{\prime},\beta,\gamma})\leq\gamma+\varepsilon^{\prime}\gamma
.
Since
G^{0}_{P^{\prime}}
is obtained from
G
by reducing the weight of all edges of
P^{\prime}
to
0
, and since the total length of
P^{\prime}
in
G
is at most
\varepsilon^{\prime}r
, we have that
\mathsf{dist}_{G\setminus\{f\}}(a_{P^{\prime},\beta},b_{P^{\prime},\beta,\gamma})\leq\gamma+\varepsilon^{\prime}\gamma+\varepsilon^{\prime}r
.
From triangle inequality we get
\mathsf{dist}_{G\setminus\{f\}}(s,b_{P^{\prime},\beta,\gamma})\leq\beta+\gamma+\varepsilon^{\prime}\gamma+\varepsilon^{\prime}r\leq\alpha+\varepsilon^{\prime}(2\alpha+\gamma+r)\leq\alpha+4\varepsilon^{\prime}r=\alpha+\varepsilon r
as required.

We are now left with the task of proving
b\leq_{P}\mathsf{first^{\alpha}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(s,P)
.
Let
b^{*}=\mathsf{first^{\alpha}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(s,P)
and let
R
be a shortest path from
s
to
b^{*}
in
G\setminus\{f\}
.
Let
a^{*}
be the first vertex on
R
that is also in
Q
.
If there is no
a^{*}
, let
b^{\prime}
be the first vertex on
R
that is on
P
and let
\beta^{*}=\min\{x\in\Gamma\mid x\geq\mathsf{len}(R[s,b^{\prime}]\}
and
\gamma^{*}=\min\{x\in\Gamma\mid x\geq\mathsf{len}(R[b^{\prime},b^{*}]\}
.
Note that
\beta^{*}+\gamma^{*}\leq(1+2\varepsilon^{\prime})\alpha
and therefore the decoding algorithm computed some vertex
b^{\beta^{*},\gamma^{*}}_{Q}
as a candidate for
b
.
Recall that
b^{\beta^{*},\gamma^{*}}_{Q}
is an
\mathsf{\varepsilon^{\prime}\gamma\text{-}first^{\gamma}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(b^{\beta^{*}}_{Q},P)
vertex for
b^{\beta^{*}}_{Q}=\mathsf{first^{\beta^{*}}_{G^{\prime}_{Q}}}(s,P)
.
Note that
R[s,b^{\prime}]
is a path with length at most
\beta^{*}
that is completely contained in
G^{\prime}_{Q}
.
Therefore,
b^{\beta^{*}}_{Q}=\mathsf{first^{\beta^{*}}_{G^{\prime}_{Q}}}(s,P)\leq_{P}b^{\prime}
.
Since
P[b^{\beta^{*}}_{Q},b^{\prime}]\cdot R[b^{\prime},b^{*}]
is a path of length at most
\gamma^{*}
in
G\setminus\{f\}
, we also have
b^{\beta^{*},\gamma^{*}}_{Q}\leq_{P}\mathsf{first^{\gamma^{*}}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(b^{\prime},P)\leq_{P}b^{*}
.
This concludes the case where
a^{*}
does not exist, since we have
b\leq_{P}b^{*}
due to
b
being the
\leq_{P}
minimum in a set containing
b^{\beta^{*},\gamma^{*}}_{Q}
.

We now treat the case where
a^{*}
exists.
Let
P^{\prime}
be the subpath of
Q
that contains
a^{*}
.
Let
\beta^{*}=\min\{x\in\Gamma\mid x\geq\mathsf{len}(R[s,a^{*}])\}
and
\gamma^{*}=\min\{x\in\Gamma\mid x\geq\mathsf{len}(R[a^{*},b^{*}])\}
, and notice that
\beta^{*}+\gamma^{*}\leq\mathsf{len}(R)+2\varepsilon^{\prime}\alpha\leq(1+2\varepsilon^{\prime})\alpha
.
Therefore, the decoding algorithm computed some
b_{P^{\prime},\beta^{*},\gamma^{*}}
that is
\mathsf{\varepsilon^{\prime}\gamma^{*}\text{-}first^{\gamma^{*}}_{G^{0}_{P^{\prime}}\setminus\{f\}}}(a_{P^{\prime},\beta^{*}},P)
with
a_{P^{\prime},\beta^{*}}=\mathsf{first^{\beta^{*}}_{G^{\prime}_{Q}}}(s,P^{\prime})
.

Since
R[s,a^{*}]
is a path from
s
to
P^{\prime}
in
G^{\prime}_{Q}
of length at most
\beta^{*}
, we have
a_{P^{\prime},\beta^{*}}\leq_{P^{\prime}}a^{*}
.
Since
P^{\prime}[a_{P^{\prime},\beta^{*}},a^{*}]\cdot R[a^{*},b^{*}]
is a path from
a_{P^{\prime},\beta^{*}}
to
b^{*}
in
G^{0}_{P^{\prime}}\setminus\{f\}
with length at most
\gamma^{*}
(recall that the length of
P^{\prime}
in
G^{0}_{P^{\prime}}
is zero), we have
b_{P^{\prime},\beta^{*},\gamma^{*}}\leq_{P}b^{*}
.
Due to the minimality of the returned vertex
b
on
P
across all choices of
P^{\prime}
,
\beta
and
\gamma
, we have that
b\leq_{P}b_{P^{\prime},\beta^{*},\gamma^{*}}\leq_{P}b^{*}
as required.
∎

5.3
The
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{s\xrightarrow{}P\setminus\{f\}}}
labeling

In this section we provide a labeling scheme
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{s\xrightarrow{}P\setminus\{f\}}}
, proving
Lemma

5.5
.
Our labeling scheme makes use of
Lemma

5.6
and the following two auxiliary labeling schemes:

Lemma 5.8
.

There exists a labeling scheme
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P\rightarrow P\setminus f}}=\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P\rightarrow P\setminus f}}_{G,P,\alpha,\varepsilon}
where
G
is a planar graph,
P
is a
0
-length path of
G
whose two endpoints lie on the same face and
\alpha,\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
.
Given the labels of two vertices
b
and
f
on
P
let
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
be the prefix and suffix of
P
before and after
f
(without
f
), respectively.
One can compute the indices on
P
of some vertices
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
that are
\mathsf{\varepsilon\alpha\text{-}first^{\alpha}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(b,P_{1})
and
\mathsf{\varepsilon\alpha\text{-}first^{\alpha}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(b,P_{2})
, respectively.
The size of each label is
\tilde{O}(\mathsf{poly}(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
.

Lemma 5.9
.

There exists a labeling scheme

\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P^{\prime}\rightarrow P\setminus f}}=\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P^{\prime}\rightarrow P\setminus f}}_{H,P,P^{\prime},\alpha,\varepsilon}
where
H
is a planar graphs
H
with a
0
-length path
P^{\prime}
and a path
P
without outgoing edges which lies on a single face, such that
P\cap P^{\prime}=\emptyset
, and
\alpha,\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
.
For
f\in P
let
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
be the prefix and suffix of
P
before and after
f
(without
f
), respectively.
Given the labels of two vertices
a\in P^{\prime}
and
f\in P
, one can retrieve two vertices
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
which are
\mathsf{\varepsilon\alpha\text{-}first^{\alpha}_{H\setminus\{f\}}}(a,P_{1})
and
\mathsf{\varepsilon\alpha\text{-}first^{\alpha}_{H\setminus\{f\}}}(a,P_{2})
, respectively.
The size of each label is
\tilde{O}(\mathsf{poly}(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
.

The proof of
Lemma

5.8
appears in
Section

6
and the proof of
Lemma

5.9
appears in
Section

7
.
We note that given
Lemmas

5.6
,
5.9
and
5.8
the label of
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{s\xrightarrow{}P\setminus\{f\}}}
is conceptually simple as we explain in the following high-level overview.
Due to certain technical details, the complete proof presented below appears more intricate.

Figure 15:
An lustration of a path from
s
to
b_{1}=\mathsf{first^{\alpha}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(s,P_{1})
.
The blue subpath is from
s
to
a\in P^{\prime}
, and the gray subpath is from
a
to
c\in P_{2}
and is internally disjoint from
P
, and the green subpath is from
c
to
b_{1}\in P_{1}
.
We use
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{s\rightarrow P^{\prime}}}
to find a good middle point for
a
on
P^{\prime}
, and
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P^{\prime}\rightarrow P\setminus f}}
to find a good middle point for
c
on
P_{2}
and
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P\rightarrow P\setminus f}}
to find a good middle point for
b_{1}
on
P_{1}
.

Overview (see
Figure

15
).

Let
R
be a shortest path from
s
to
b_{1}=\mathsf{first^{\alpha}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(s,P_{1})
in
G\setminus\{f\}
, and let
Q
be a separator that separates
s
and
f
.
We are interested in two special vertices on
R
: the first vertex
a^{*}
on
R
that is in
Q
, and specifically on some
P^{\prime}
which is an
\varepsilon r
-subpath of
Q
, and the first vertex
c^{*}
on
R
after
a^{*}
that is on
P
(say, on
P_{2}
).
We ’guess’ estimations
\beta,\gamma,\delta
for the lengths of
R[s,a^{*}]
,
R[a^{*},c^{*}]
and
R[c^{*},b_{1}]
, respectively.
In this overview, it is useful to consider a vertex
m
that is
\mathsf{\varepsilon r\text{-}first^{\beta}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(s,P)
as a sufficiently good ’middle point.
Specifically, if one aims to reach an early vertex on
P
from
s
within a budget
\alpha
, requiring the path to pass through
m
and allowing the budget to exceed
\alpha
by
\varepsilon r
does not worsen the result on
P
.
Using the labels
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{s\rightarrow P^{\prime}}}
with budget
\beta
, we find a sufficiently good ’middle point’
a
for replacing
a^{*}
.
Starting from
a
, we use the labels
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P^{\prime}\rightarrow P\setminus f}}
with budget
\gamma
to find a sufficiently good ’middle point’ replacing
c^{*}
.
Finally, we use
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P\rightarrow P\setminus f}}
with budget
\delta
to find a vertex earlier on
P
than
\mathsf{first^{\gamma}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(c^{*},P_{1})
.

Since each middle point is reachable with a budget increase of
O(\varepsilon r)
, the final point is reachable with a budget increase of
O(\varepsilon r)
.
Since every middle point is ’reasonably good’, and the budget used in each phase is an estimation of the length of the corresponding subpath of
R
, it is guaranteed that the final destination is at least as good as
b_{1}
.

We are now ready to provide the formal proof of
Lemma

5.5
.

See
5.5

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon^{\prime}=\frac{\varepsilon}{7}\in\Theta(\varepsilon)
be an approximation parameter and let
\Gamma=\{i\varepsilon^{\prime}\alpha\mid i\in[\left\lceil{\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{\prime}}}\right\rceil]\}
.
For a subpath
P^{\prime}
of a separator in
\mathcal{T}
below
P
, and for
\beta,\gamma,\delta\in\Gamma
we define the following graphs.

1.

G^{P}_{P^{\prime},\delta}
is the graph
G
where the weights of all edges of
P^{\prime}
are set to
0
, and the label of each vertex
v
is set to be
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P\rightarrow P\setminus f}}_{G,P,\delta,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(v)
obtained by
Lemma

5.8
.

2.

G^{0}_{P^{\prime},\delta}
is the graph obtained from
G^{P}_{P^{\prime},\delta}
by removing all outgoing edges of
P
(the edges of
P
itself are not removed).

3.

G^{1}_{P^{\prime},\delta,\gamma}
is the graph obtained by setting the label of each vertex
v
in
G^{0}_{P^{\prime},\delta}
to be
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P^{\prime}\rightarrow P\setminus f}}_{G^{0}_{P^{\prime},\delta},P,P^{\prime},\gamma,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(v)
.

4.

G^{2}_{P^{\prime},\delta,\gamma}
is the induced graph of
G^{1}_{P^{\prime},\delta,\gamma}
only on vertices that are below the separator of
P^{\prime}
in the fully recursive decomposition.

The Labeling.

For every vertex
v
below the separator of
P
in the fully recursive decomposition the label of
v
stores

the index of
v
in
P
, if
v\in P
.
For every tuple
(P^{\prime},\beta,\gamma,\delta)
such that
P^{\prime}
is an
\varepsilon^{\prime}r
-subpath ancestor of
v
and
\beta,\gamma,\delta\in\Gamma
, the label
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{s\rightarrow P^{\prime}}}_{G^{2}_{P^{\prime},\delta,\gamma},P^{\prime},\beta}(v)
and the labels of
v
in
G^{2}_{P^{\prime},\delta,\gamma}
and in
G^{P}_{P^{\prime},\delta}
.
Moreover, the label of
v
stores the identifiers of the ancestor pieces of
v
in
\mathcal{T}
.
Finally, for every maximal consecutive subpath
P^{*}
of
P
in
G^{\prime}_{P^{\prime}}
, and
\gamma,\delta\in\Gamma
the label of
v
stores
b^{\gamma}_{Q,P^{*}}=\mathsf{first^{\gamma}_{G^{\prime}_{P^{\prime}}}}(v,P^{*})
where
Q
is the separator from which
P^{\prime}
originates and
G^{\prime}_{P^{\prime}}
is the subgraph below
Q
, and the label
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P\rightarrow P\setminus f}}_{G,P,\gamma,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(b^{\gamma}_{Q,P^{*}})
.

Size.

There are
O(\log n)
ancestor separators of
v
in
\mathcal{T}
.
Each separator is partitioned into
O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon})
subpaths, so overall there are
\tilde{O}(\mathsf{poly}(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
options for
P^{\prime}
for each vertex
v
.
It follows from
|\Gamma|=O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon})
that there are
\tilde{O}(\mathsf{poly}(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
tuples
(P^{\prime},\beta,\gamma,\delta)
.
For every such tuple, the label stores an
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{s\rightarrow P^{\prime}}}
-type label in
G^{2}_{P^{\prime},\delta,\gamma}
and the label of
v
in
G^{2}_{P^{\prime},\delta,\gamma}
and in
G^{P}_{\delta}
.
Due to
Lemma

5.6
, the size of
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{s\rightarrow P^{\prime}}}
is
\tilde{O}(\mathsf{poly}(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
. In our construction, the size should be multiplied by another two
\tilde{O}(\mathsf{poly}(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
factors since the
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{s\rightarrow P^{\prime}}}
label is applied to the graph
G^{1}_{P^{\prime},\delta,\gamma}
in which every vertex is labeled with a
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P^{\prime}\rightarrow P\setminus f}}
type label (which is of size
\tilde{O}(\mathsf{poly}(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
due to
Lemma

5.9
), and these
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P^{\prime}\rightarrow P\setminus f}}
labels are applied to the graph
G^{P}_{P^{\prime},\delta}
in which the label of each vertex is a
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P\rightarrow P\setminus f}}
-type label (which is of size
\tilde{O}(\mathsf{poly}(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
due to
Lemma

5.8
).
Finally, the height of the recursive decomposition is logarithmic, and in every ancestor piece of
v
below
P
, the number of maximal consecutive subpaths of
P
is bounded by the number of apices ancestors of
v
which is
\tilde{O}(1)
.
It follows that storing for every ancestor piece of
v
, for every maximal consecutive subpath
P^{*}
of
P
in the piece and for every
\gamma,\delta\in\Gamma
the vertex
b^{\gamma}_{Q,P^{*}}
and an
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P\rightarrow P\setminus f}}
-type label of
b^{\gamma}_{Q,P^{*}}
increases the size of the label by
\tilde{O}(\mathsf{poly}(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
.

Decoding.

Given the labels of two vertices
s
and
f\in P
that are not an ancestor apex of one another, and such that
P
is an ancestor path of both
s
and
f
, the algorithm finds a vertex
b_{1}
that is
\mathsf{\varepsilon r\text{-}first^{\alpha}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(s,P_{1})
and a vertex
b_{2}
that is
\mathsf{\varepsilon r\text{-}first^{\alpha}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(s,P_{2})
as follows.
The algorithm starts by finding the highest separator
Q
in the recursive decomposition that has
s
in one side, and
f
strictly in the other side (this can be done since both
s
and
f
store all ancestor pieces).
Recall that
Q
is partitioned into
O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon})
subpaths of length at most
\varepsilon^{\prime}r
each.
For each such subpath
P^{\prime}
, and for every
\beta,\gamma,\delta\in\Gamma
such that
\beta+\gamma+\delta\leq(1+3\varepsilon^{\prime})\alpha
, the algorithm uses
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{s\rightarrow P^{\prime}}}_{G^{2}_{P^{\prime},\delta,\gamma},P^{\prime},\beta}(s)
to find
a_{P^{\prime},\beta}=\mathsf{first^{\beta}_{G^{2}_{P^{\prime},\delta,\gamma}}}(s,P^{\prime})
.
More precisely, the label
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{s\rightarrow P^{\prime}}}_{G^{2}_{P^{\prime},\delta,\gamma},P^{\prime},\beta}(s)
allows the algorithm to retrieve
\ell_{a}=\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P^{\prime}\rightarrow P\setminus f}}_{G^{0}_{P^{\prime},\delta},P,P^{\prime},\gamma,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(a_{P^{\prime},\beta})
.
Note that the label of
f
contains
\ell_{f}=\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P^{\prime}\xrightarrow{f}P}}_{G^{0}_{P^{\prime},\delta},P,P^{\prime},\gamma,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(f)
.
The algorithm uses the labels
\ell_{a}
and
\ell_{f}
to retrieve vertices
b^{1}_{P^{\prime},\beta,\gamma}
and
b^{2}_{P^{\prime},\beta,\gamma}
on
P_{1}
(resp. on
P_{2}
) such that
b^{1}_{P^{\prime},\beta,\gamma}
is an
\mathsf{\varepsilon^{\prime}\gamma\text{-}first^{\gamma}_{G^{0}_{P^{\prime},\delta}\setminus\{f\}}}(a_{P^{\prime},\beta},P_{1})
and
b^{2}_{P^{\prime},\beta,\gamma}
is an
\mathsf{\varepsilon^{\prime}\gamma\text{-}first^{\gamma}_{G^{0}_{P^{\prime},\delta}\setminus\{f\}}}(a_{P^{\prime},\beta},P_{2})
.
Again, since the vertices of the graph
G^{0}_{P^{\prime},\delta}
are labeled, the algorithm actually obtains
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P\rightarrow P\setminus f}}_{G,P,\delta,\varepsilon^{\prime}}
labels of
b^{1}_{P^{\prime},\beta,\gamma}
and of
b^{2}_{P^{\prime},\beta,\gamma}
, denote these labels as
\ell^{1}_{b}
and
\ell^{2}_{b}
respectively.
Recall that the label of
f
stores
\ell^{\prime}_{f}=\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P\rightarrow P\setminus f}}_{G,P,\delta,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(f)
.
The algorithm uses the labels
\ell^{1}_{b}
,
\ell^{2}_{b}
and
\ell^{\prime}_{f}
to obtain:

•

a vertex
b^{1,1}_{P^{\prime},\beta,\gamma,\delta}
that is
\mathsf{\varepsilon^{\prime}\delta\text{-}first^{\delta}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(b^{1}_{P^{\prime},\beta,\gamma},P_{1})
,

•

a vertex
b^{1,2}_{P^{\prime},\beta,\gamma,\delta}
that is
\mathsf{\varepsilon^{\prime}\delta\text{-}first^{\delta}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(b^{1}_{P^{\prime},\beta,\gamma},P_{2})
,

•

a vertex
b^{2,1}_{P^{\prime},\beta,\gamma,\delta}
that is
\mathsf{\varepsilon^{\prime}\delta\text{-}first^{\delta}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(b^{2}_{P^{\prime},\beta,\gamma},P_{1})
,

•

a vertex
b^{2,2}_{P^{\prime},\beta,\gamma,\delta}
that is
\mathsf{\varepsilon^{\prime}\delta\text{-}first^{\delta}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(b^{2}_{P^{\prime},\beta,\gamma},P_{2})
.

Let
b^{\gamma}_{Q,1}=\min_{\leq P}\{b^{\gamma}_{Q,P^{*}}\mid P^{*}\textbf{ is a maximal consecutive subpath of }P\textbf{ in }G^{\prime}_{Q}\textit{, }b^{\gamma}_{Q,P^{*}}\in P_{1}\}
and
b^{\gamma}_{Q,2}=\min_{\leq P}\{b^{\gamma}_{Q,P^{*}}\mid P^{*}\textbf{ is a maximal consecutive subpath of }P\textbf{ in }G^{\prime}_{Q}\textit{, }b^{\gamma}_{Q,P^{*}}\in P_{2}\}
.
Notice that
b^{\gamma}_{Q,1}
and
b^{\gamma}_{Q,2}
can be computed using the index of
f
in
P
to classify each
b^{\gamma}_{Q,P^{*}}
either to
P_{1}
or to
P_{2}
.
The algorithm uses the labels
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P\rightarrow P\setminus f}}_{G,P,\delta,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(b^{\gamma}_{Q,1})
,
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P\rightarrow P\setminus f}}_{G,P,\delta,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(b^{\gamma}_{Q,1})
, and
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P\rightarrow P\setminus f}}_{G,P,\delta,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(f)
to obtain the vertices:

1.

b^{\gamma,\delta}_{Q,1,1}
that is an
\mathsf{\varepsilon^{\prime}\delta\text{-}first^{\delta}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(b^{\gamma}_{Q,1},P_{1})
.

2.

b^{\gamma,\delta}_{Q,1,2}
that is an
\mathsf{\varepsilon^{\prime}\delta\text{-}first^{\delta}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(b^{\gamma}_{Q,1},P_{2})
.

3.

b^{\gamma,\delta}_{Q,2,1}
that is an
\mathsf{\varepsilon^{\prime}\delta\text{-}first^{\delta}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(b^{\gamma}_{Q,2},P_{1})
.

4.

b^{\gamma,\delta}_{Q,2,2}
that is an
\mathsf{\varepsilon^{\prime}\delta\text{-}first^{\delta}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(b^{\gamma}_{Q,2},P_{2})
.

The algorithm sets the vertex
b_{1}=\min_{\leq P}\big(\{b^{x,1}_{P^{\prime},\beta,\gamma,\delta}\mid\beta,\gamma,\delta\in\Gamma,P^{\prime}\textit{ is a subpath of }Q,\beta+\gamma+\delta\leq(1+3\varepsilon^{\prime})\alpha,x\in\{1,2\}\}\cup\{b^{\gamma,\delta}_{Q,x,1}\mid\gamma+\delta\leq(1+2\varepsilon^{\prime})\alpha,x\in\{1,2\}\}\big)
i.e. the first vertex on
P_{1}
that was found across all choices of
\beta,\gamma,\delta
and a subpath
P^{\prime}
, or some
b^{\gamma,\delta}_{Q,x,1}
.
The algorithm also sets
b_{2}=\min_{\leq P}\big(\{b^{x,2}_{\beta,\gamma,\delta}\mid\beta,\gamma,\delta\in\Gamma,P^{\prime}\textit{ is a subpath of }Q,\beta+\gamma+\delta\leq(1+3\varepsilon^{\prime})\alpha,x\in\{1,2\}\}\cup\{b^{\gamma,\delta}_{Q,x,2}\mid\gamma+\delta\leq(1+2\varepsilon^{\prime})\alpha,x\in\{1,2\}\}\big)
.
That is, the first vertex on
P_{2}
that was found across all guesses of
\beta,\gamma,\delta
and a subpath
P^{\prime}
of
Q
, or
b^{\gamma,\delta}_{Q,x,2}
.
The algorithm returns
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
.

Correctness.

We show that when decoding the labels of two vertices
s
and
f
, we indeed return a vertex
b_{1}
that is
\mathsf{\varepsilon r\text{-}first^{\alpha}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(s,P_{1})
.
The proof that
b_{2}
is
\mathsf{\varepsilon r\text{-}first^{\alpha}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(s,P_{2})
is similar.
We start by showing that for every candidate
b^{\prime}
the algorithm considers for being
b_{1}
, we have
\mathsf{dist}_{G\setminus\{f\}}(s,b^{\prime})\leq\alpha+\varepsilon r
.

Let
Q
be the lowest separator in the decomposition such that
s
is in one side of
Q
and
f
is strictly in the other side, and let
P^{*}
be a maximal consecutive subpath of
P
contained in
G^{\prime}_{Q}
.
We start by focusing of a candidate
b^{\prime}
of the form
b^{\gamma,\delta}_{Q,x,1}
.
Recall that
\gamma+\delta\leq(1+2\varepsilon^{\prime})\alpha
and that
b^{\gamma,\delta}_{Q,x,1}
is an
\mathsf{\varepsilon^{\prime}\gamma\text{-}first^{\gamma}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(b^{\gamma}_{Q,x},P_{1})
vertex for
b^{\gamma}_{Q,x}
which is the first vertex of
P
that can be reached on
P_{1}
in
G^{\prime}_{Q}
with budget
\gamma
.
By definition, we have
\mathsf{dist}_{G^{\prime}_{Q}}(s,b^{\gamma}_{Q,1})\leq\gamma
, and since
f\notin G^{\prime}_{Q}
we have
\mathsf{dist}_{G\setminus\{f\}}(s,b^{\gamma}_{Q,x})\leq\gamma
.
We also have
\mathsf{dist}_{G\setminus\{f\}}(b^{\gamma}_{Q,x},b^{\gamma,\delta}_{Q,x,1})\leq(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})\delta
.
It follows from triangle inequality that
\mathsf{dist}_{G\setminus\{f\}}(s,b^{\gamma,\delta}_{Q,x,1})\leq\gamma+(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})\delta\leq\alpha+3\varepsilon^{\prime}\alpha\leq(1+\varepsilon)\alpha
as required.

We now treat candidates of the form
b^{x,1}_{P^{\prime},\beta,\gamma,\delta}
for some
\beta,\gamma,\delta\in\Gamma
and
x\in\{1,2\}
.
Recall that:

1.

b^{x,1}_{P^{\prime},\beta,\gamma,\delta}
is a
\mathsf{\varepsilon^{\prime}\delta\text{-}first^{\delta}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(b^{x}_{P^{\prime},\beta,\gamma},P_{1})
.

2.

b^{x}_{P^{\prime},\beta,\gamma}
is a
\mathsf{\varepsilon^{\prime}\gamma\text{-}first^{\gamma}_{G^{0}_{P^{\prime},\delta}\setminus\{f\}}}(a_{P^{\prime},\beta},P_{x})
.

3.

a_{P^{\prime},\beta}
is
\mathsf{first^{\beta}_{G^{2}_{P^{\prime},\delta,\gamma}}}(s,P^{\prime})

Recall that
f\notin G^{2}_{P^{\prime},\delta,\gamma}
since
G^{2}_{P^{\prime},\delta,\gamma}
only contains vertices in the side of
s
of the separator of
Q
in the full recursive decomposition.
Also notice that in
G^{2}_{P^{\prime},\delta,\gamma}
the length of the path
P^{\prime}
is
0
, and in
G
the length of
P^{\prime}
is at most
\varepsilon^{\prime}r
.
It follows that
\mathsf{dist}_{G\setminus\{f\}}(s,a_{P^{\prime},\beta})\leq\mathsf{dist}_{G^{2}_{P^{\prime},\delta,\gamma}}(s,a_{P^{\prime},\beta})+\varepsilon^{\prime}r\leq\beta+\varepsilon^{\prime}r
.
Due to the same reasoning, we also have
\mathsf{dist}_{G\setminus\{f\}}(a_{P^{\prime},\beta},b^{x}_{P^{\prime},\beta,\gamma})\leq\mathsf{dist}_{G^{0}_{P^{\prime},\delta}\setminus\{f\}}(a_{P,\beta},b^{x}_{P^{\prime},\beta,\gamma})+\varepsilon^{\prime}r\leq\gamma+\varepsilon^{\prime}\gamma+\varepsilon^{\prime}r
.
Directly from the definition of
\mathsf{\varepsilon^{\prime}\delta\text{-}first^{\delta}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(b^{x}_{P^{\prime},\beta,\gamma},P_{1})
we have
\mathsf{dist}_{G\setminus\{f\}}(b^{x}_{P^{\prime},\beta,\gamma},b^{x,1}_{P^{\prime},\beta,\gamma,\delta})\leq\delta+\varepsilon^{\prime}\delta
.

From triangle inequality and
\beta+\gamma+\delta\leq(1+3\varepsilon^{\prime})\alpha
we get
\mathsf{dist}_{G\setminus\{f\}}(s,b^{x,1}_{P^{\prime},\beta,\gamma,\delta})\leq(\beta+\varepsilon^{\prime}r)+(\gamma+\varepsilon^{\prime}\gamma+\varepsilon^{\prime}r)+(\delta+\varepsilon^{\prime}\delta)\leq(\beta+\gamma+\delta)+\varepsilon^{\prime}(2r+\gamma+\delta)\leq\alpha+\varepsilon^{\prime}(2r+3\alpha+\gamma+\delta)\leq\alpha+7\varepsilon^{\prime}r=\alpha+\varepsilon r
as required.

We are now left with the task of proving
b_{1}\leq_{P}\mathsf{first^{\alpha}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(s,P_{1})
.
Notice that we have
b_{1}\in P_{1}
since
b_{1}
can either be
b_{Q,1}
which is by definition on
P_{1}
, or
b^{x,1}_{\beta,\gamma,\delta}
which is a
P_{1}
output of a
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P\rightarrow P\setminus f}}
-type label.

Let
b^{*}=\mathsf{first^{\alpha}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(s,P_{1})
and let
R
be a shortest path from
s
to
b^{*}
in
G\setminus\{f\}
.
Let
a^{*}
be the first vertex on
R
that is also in
Q
, and let
c^{*}
be the first vertex on
R[a^{*}..b^{*}]
that is on
P
.
Notice that
a^{*}
(and therefore,
c^{*}
) do not necessarily exist, but if
a^{*}
exists, so does
c^{*}
.

Case 1:
a^{*}
does not exist

Let
b^{\prime}
be the first vertex on
R
that is on
P
.
Let
\gamma^{*}=\min\{x\in\Gamma\mid x\geq\mathsf{len}(R[s,b^{\prime}]\}
and
\delta^{*}=\min\{x\in\Gamma\mid x\geq\mathsf{len}(R[b^{\prime},b^{*}]\}
and
b^{\prime}\in P_{x}
for
x\in\{1,2\}
.
Notice that
b^{\prime}
must be on one of the maximal consecutive subpaths of
P
that are contained in
G^{\prime}_{Q}
, we denote this maximal subpath as
P^{*}
.
Since
R[s,b^{\prime}]
is a path with length at most
\gamma
in
G^{\prime}_{Q}
from
s
to
b^{\prime}
, we have
b^{\gamma^{*}}_{Q,P^{*}}=\mathsf{first^{\gamma}_{G^{\prime}_{Q}}}(s,P^{*})\leq_{P}b^{\prime}
.
In particular,
b^{\gamma^{*}}_{Q,x}\leq_{P}b^{\prime}
as an
\leq_{P}
minimum in a set containing
P^{*}
.
Notice that
\gamma^{*}+\delta^{*}\leq(1+2\varepsilon^{\prime})\alpha
, so the algorithm considered a candidate
b^{\gamma^{*},\delta^{*}}_{Q,x,1}
that is an
\mathsf{\varepsilon^{\prime}\delta^{*}\text{-}first^{\delta^{*}}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(b^{\gamma^{*}}_{Q,x},P_{1})
.
Since
P_{1}[b^{\gamma^{*}}_{Q,x},b^{\prime}]R[b^{\prime},b^{*}]
is a path in
G\setminus\{f\}
from
b^{\gamma^{*}}_{Q,x}
to
b^{*}
of length at most
\delta^{*}
, we have that
b^{\gamma^{*},\delta^{*}}_{Q,x,1}\leq_{P}\mathsf{first^{\gamma^{*}}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(b^{\gamma^{*}}_{Q,x},P_{1})\leq_{P}b^{*}
.

This concludes the proof of this case, as
b_{1}\leq_{P}b^{\gamma^{*},\delta^{*}}_{Q,x,1}
as the
\leq_{P}
minimum of a set containing the latter.

Case 2:
a^{*}
exists, and
c^{*}\in P_{1}

Let
P^{\prime}
be the subpath of
Q
that contains
a^{*}
.
Let:

1.

\beta^{*}=\min\{x\in\Gamma\mid x\geq\mathsf{len}(R[s,a^{*}])\}
,

2.

\gamma^{*}=\min\{x\in\Gamma\mid x\geq\mathsf{len}(R[a^{*},c^{*}])\}
, and

3.

\delta^{*}=\min\{x\in\Gamma\mid x\geq\mathsf{len}(R[c^{*},b^{*}])\}
.

Notice that
\beta^{*}+\gamma^{*}+\delta^{*}\leq\mathsf{len}(R)+3\varepsilon^{\prime}\alpha
.
Therefore, the decoding algorithm computed some
b^{1,1}_{\beta^{*},\gamma^{*},\delta^{*}}
such that:

1.

b^{1,1}_{\beta^{*},\gamma^{*},\delta^{*}}
is a
\mathsf{\varepsilon^{\prime}\delta^{*}\text{-}first^{\delta^{*}}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(b^{1}_{\beta^{*},\gamma^{*}},P_{1})
.

2.

b^{1}_{\beta^{*},\gamma^{*}}
is a
\mathsf{\varepsilon^{\prime}\gamma^{*}\text{-}first^{\gamma^{*}}_{G^{0}_{P^{\prime},\delta^{*}}\setminus\{f\}}}(a_{\beta^{*}},P_{1})
.

3.

a_{\beta^{*}}
is
\mathsf{first^{\beta^{*}}_{G^{2}_{P^{\prime},\delta^{*},\gamma^{*}}}}(s,P^{\prime})
.

Notice that we omit
P^{\prime}
from the subscript of the vertices listed above (i.e.
b^{1,1}_{P^{\prime},\beta^{*},\gamma^{*},\delta^{*}}
is written as
b^{1,1}_{\beta^{*},\gamma^{*},\delta^{*}}
). This is done for convenience, as this notation will not be used in the current context with a subpath other than
P^{\prime}
.

Recall that
G^{2}_{P^{\prime},\gamma^{*},\delta^{*}}
is a vertex labeled version of
G^{\prime}_{Q}
, with the weights of the edges of
P^{\prime}
set to
0
.
Since
R[s,a^{*}]
is a path from
s
to
P^{\prime}
in
G^{\prime}_{Q}\setminus\{f\}=G^{\prime}_{Q}
with length at most
\beta^{*}
, it is in particular a path from
s
to
a^{*}\in P^{\prime}
in
G^{2}_{P^{\prime},\gamma^{*},\delta^{*}}
with length at most
\beta^{*}
and therefore we have
a_{\beta^{*}}\leq_{P^{\prime}}a^{*}
.

Recall that
G^{0}_{P^{\prime},\delta^{*}}
is a vertex labeled version of
G
with the weights of all edges of
P^{\prime}
set to
0
without outgoing edges from
P
.
Since
R[a^{*},c^{*}]
is a path from
a^{*}
to
c^{*}
in
G\setminus\{f\}
with length at most
\gamma^{*}
that do not use outgoing edges of
P
(as it is internally disjoint from
P
), we have that
P^{\prime}[a_{\beta^{*}},a^{*}]\cdot R[a^{*},c^{*}]
is a path from
a_{\beta^{*}}
to
c^{*}\in P_{1}
in
G^{0}_{P^{\prime},\delta^{*}}\setminus\{f\}
with length at most
\gamma^{*}
(also recall that
f\notin P^{\prime}
).
We therefore have
b^{1}_{\beta^{*},\gamma^{*}}\leq_{P}c^{*}
.

Furthermore, consider the path
P_{1}[b^{1}_{\beta^{*},\gamma^{*}},c^{*}]\cdot R[c^{*},b^{*}]
.
It is a path with length at most
\delta^{*}
(recall that
\mathsf{len}(P)=0
) in
G\setminus\{f\}
.
Therefore, we have
b^{1,1}_{\beta^{*},\gamma^{*},\delta^{*}}\leq_{P}b^{*}
.
Due to the minimality of the returned vertex
b_{1}
on
P
across all values of
\beta,\gamma,\delta
and all subpaths
P^{\prime}
, we have that
b_{1}\leq_{P}b^{1,1}_{\beta^{*},\gamma^{*},\delta^{*}}\leq_{P}b^{*}
as required.

Case 3:
a^{*}
exists and
c^{*}\in P_{2}

This proof for this case is completely identical to the proof of the previous case, replacing
b^{1,1}_{\beta^{*},\gamma^{*},\delta^{*}}
with
b^{2,1}_{\beta^{*},\gamma^{*},\delta^{*}}
.
∎

6
The
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P\rightarrow P\setminus f}}
Labeling (Proof of
Lemma

5.8
)

In this section we prove
Lemma

5.8
by extending the ideas of
Section

4.4
from reachability to approximate distances.
We recall the settings of
Lemma

5.8
.

G
is a planar graph,
P
is a
0
-length path of
G
whose two endpoints lie on the same face and
\alpha,\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
. Our goal is to assign
\tilde{O}(\mathsf{poly}(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
-sized labels to the vertices of
P
such that, given the labels of two vertices
b
and
f
of
P
, one can compute the indices on
P
of some vertices
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
that are
\mathsf{\varepsilon\alpha\text{-}first^{\alpha}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(b,P_{1})
and
\mathsf{\varepsilon\alpha\text{-}first^{\alpha}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(b,P_{2})
, respectively. Throughout,
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
denote the prefix and suffix of
P
before and after vertex
f
(without
f
) respectively.

Figure 16:
An illustration of
G_{1}
.
We are interested in
P_{2}
to
P_{1}
paths (like the blue path) that may start with some edges of
P_{2}
and then continue with a subpath which is internally disjoint from
P
.
Formally, this is achieved by removing all in-going edges to
P_{2}
and all out-going edges from
P_{1}
(the removed edges are displayed in gray in the figure).

We define
G_{1}
(resp.
G_{2}
) to be the graph obtained from
G\setminus\{f\}
by first removing all in-going edges to vertices of
P_{2}
(resp.
P_{1}
), except for the edges of
P_{2}
(resp.
P_{1}
) itself, and then removing all out-going edges from vertices of
P_{1}
(resp.
P_{2}
), including the edges of
P_{1}
(resp.
P_{2}
), see
Figure

16
.
Intuitively,
G_{1}
(resp.
G_{2}
) is a version of
G\setminus\{f\}
in which every path from
P_{2}
(resp.
P_{1}
) to
P_{1}
(resp.
P_{2}
) starts with some subpath of
P_{2}
(resp.
P_{1}
) and is then internally disjoint from
P
.

We divide our task into four auxiliary labeling schemes.

1.

\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P_{2}\rightarrow P_{1}\rightarrow P}}_{\beta,\gamma}
- Given the labels of two vertices
f<_{P}b
, one can obtain
\mathsf{first^{\gamma}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(x,P_{1})
and
\mathsf{first^{\gamma}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(x,P_{2})
for a vertex
x
that is
\mathsf{\varepsilon\beta\text{-}first^{\beta}_{G_{1}}}(b,P_{1})
.
We introduce a labeling scheme for this problem in
Section

6.1
.

2.

\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P_{1}\rightarrow P_{2}\rightarrow P}}_{\beta,\gamma}
- Given the labels of two vertices
b<_{P}f
, one can obtain
\mathsf{first^{\gamma}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(x,P_{1})
and
\mathsf{first^{\gamma}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(x,P_{2})
for a vertex
x
that is
\mathsf{\varepsilon\beta\text{-}first^{\beta}_{G_{2}}}(b,P_{2})
.
The labeling scheme of
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P_{2}\rightarrow P_{1}\rightarrow P}}_{\beta,\gamma}
(
Section

6.1
) can be adjusted to solve this problem.

3.

\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P_{2}\rightarrow P_{2}}}_{\beta}
- Given the labels of two vertices
f<_{P}b
one can compute
\mathsf{\varepsilon\beta\text{-}first^{\beta}_{G\setminus(P_{1}\cup\{f\})}}(b,P_{2})
.
We introduce a labeling scheme for this problem in
Section

6.2
.

4.

\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P_{1}\rightarrow P_{1}}}_{\beta}
- Given the labels of two vertices
b<_{P}f
one can compute
\mathsf{\varepsilon\beta\text{-}first^{\beta}_{G\setminus(P_{2}\cup\{f\})}}(b,P_{1})
.
The labeling scheme of
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P_{2}\rightarrow P_{2}}}_{\beta}
(
Section

6.2
) can be adjusted to solve this problem.

Exploiting the labeling schemes for the auxiliary tasks, we prove the following lemma (see
Figure

17
).

Figure 17:
An illustration of a path
R
from
b
to
w=\mathsf{first^{\alpha}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(b,P_{1})
.
The first subpath is from
b
to
y\in P_{2}
(without using
P_{1}
),
the second subpath is from
y
to
z\in P_{1}
using a path that is internally disjoint from
P
except for a prefix.
Finally, the third subpath from
z
to
w\in P_{1}
is a path in
G\setminus\{f\}
.
The first part is approximated using
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P_{2}\rightarrow P_{2}}}
, the second and third parts are approximated using
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P_{2}\rightarrow P_{1}\rightarrow P}}
.

See
5.8

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon^{\prime}=\varepsilon/6
and let
\Gamma=\{i\varepsilon^{\prime}\alpha\mid i\in[0,\left\lceil{1/\varepsilon^{\prime}}\right\rceil]\}
be the set of multiples of
\varepsilon^{\prime}\alpha
.
For every
(\gamma,\delta)\in\Gamma^{2}
we define the labeled graph
G_{\gamma,\delta}
as a copy of
G
in which every vertex
v\in V
is labeled with
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P_{2}\rightarrow P_{1}\rightarrow P}}_{\gamma,\delta}(v)
and
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P_{1}\rightarrow P_{2}\rightarrow P}}_{\gamma,\delta}(v)
, where the labeles are computed with approximation factor
\varepsilon^{\prime}
.

For every
v\in V
, the label
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P\rightarrow P\setminus f}}(v)
stores for every
(\beta,\gamma,\delta)\in\Gamma^{3}

the labels of
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P_{2}\rightarrow P_{2}}}_{\beta}(v)
and
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P_{1}\rightarrow P_{1}}}_{\beta}(v)
computed with approximation factor
\varepsilon^{\prime}
on the graph
G_{\gamma,\delta}
, and also the label of
v
in
G_{\gamma,\delta}
.

Size.

By
Lemmas

6.6
and
6.14
we have that the size of each label of
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P_{2}\rightarrow P_{1}\rightarrow P}},\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P_{1}\rightarrow P_{2}\rightarrow P}},\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P_{2}\rightarrow P_{2}}}
and
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P_{1}\rightarrow P_{1}}}
is
\tilde{O}(\mathsf{poly}(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
. Thus, the size of
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P\rightarrow P\setminus f}}(v)
is
\tilde{O}(\mathsf{poly}(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
.
Note that
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P_{2}\rightarrow P_{2}}}
and
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P_{1}\rightarrow P_{1}}}
labels on the graph
G_{\beta,\delta}
, in which vertices have labels of size
\tilde{O}(\mathsf{poly}(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
, still have size
\tilde{O}(\mathsf{poly}(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
.

Decoding.

Given the labels of
b
and
f
, if
f<_{P}b
the computation of the indices on
P
of some vertices
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
that are
\mathsf{\varepsilon\alpha\text{-}first^{\alpha}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(b,P_{1})
and
\mathsf{\varepsilon\alpha\text{-}first^{\alpha}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(b,P_{2})
, respectively, is done as follows.
Iterate over all triplets
(\beta,\gamma,\delta)\in\Gamma^{3}
such that
\beta+\gamma+\delta\leq(1+3\varepsilon^{\prime})\alpha
.
The algorithm uses
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P_{2}\rightarrow P_{2}}}_{\beta}(b)
and
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P_{2}\rightarrow P_{2}}}_{\beta}(f)
to compute some vertex
b^{\prime}_{\beta,\gamma,\delta}\in G_{\gamma,\delta}
which is
\mathsf{\varepsilon^{\prime}\beta\text{-}first^{\beta}_{G\setminus(P_{1}\cup\{f\})}}(b,P_{2})
.
Then, using
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P_{2}\rightarrow P_{1}\rightarrow P}}_{\gamma,\delta}(f)
and
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P_{2}\rightarrow P_{1}\rightarrow P}}_{\gamma,\delta}(b^{\prime}_{\beta,\gamma,\delta})
we compute
b^{1}_{\beta,\gamma,\delta}=\mathsf{first^{\delta}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(x_{\beta,\gamma,\delta},P_{1})
and
b^{2}_{\beta,\gamma,\delta}=\mathsf{first^{\delta}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(x_{\beta,\gamma,\delta},P_{2})
for a vertex
x_{\beta,\gamma,\delta}
that is
\mathsf{\varepsilon^{\prime}\gamma\text{-}first^{\gamma}_{G_{1}}}(b^{\prime}_{\beta,\gamma,\delta},P_{1})
.
Finally,
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
are the earliest vertices of
P
among all
b^{1}_{\beta,\gamma,\delta}
and
b^{2}_{\beta,\gamma,\delta}
, respectively.
The case where
b<_{P}f
is decoded in a similar way, using
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P_{1}\rightarrow P_{1}}}
and
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P_{1}\rightarrow P_{2}\rightarrow P}}
.

Correctness.

First notice that for every
(\beta,\gamma,\delta)
