we have
\mathsf{dist}_{G\setminus\{f\}}(b,b^{1}_{\beta,\gamma,\delta})\leq\mathsf{dist}_{G\setminus\{f\}}(b,b^{\prime}_{\beta,\gamma,\delta})+\mathsf{dist}_{G\setminus\{f\}}(b^{\prime}_{\beta,\gamma,\delta},x_{\beta,\gamma,\delta})+\mathsf{dist}_{G\setminus\{f\}}(x_{\beta,\gamma,\delta},b^{1}_{\beta,\gamma,\delta})
.
By definition of
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P_{2}\rightarrow P_{2}}}
we have
\mathsf{dist}_{G\setminus\{f\}}(b,b^{\prime}_{\beta,\gamma,\delta})\leq(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})\beta
and by definition of
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P_{2}\rightarrow P_{1}\rightarrow P}}
we have
\mathsf{dist}_{G\setminus\{f\}}(b^{\prime}_{\beta,\gamma,\delta},x_{\beta,\gamma,\delta})\leq(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})\gamma
and
\mathsf{dist}_{G\setminus\{f\}}(x_{\beta,\gamma,\delta},b^{1}_{\beta,\gamma,\delta})\leq(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})\delta
.
Thus,
\mathsf{dist}_{G\setminus\{f\}}(b,b^{1}_{\beta,\gamma,\delta})\leq(1+3\varepsilon^{\prime})\alpha+3\varepsilon^{\prime}\alpha=(1+6\varepsilon^{\prime})\alpha=(1+\varepsilon)\alpha
.
By similar arguments,
\mathsf{dist}_{G\setminus\{f\}}(b,b^{2}_{\beta,\gamma,\delta})\leq(1+6\varepsilon^{\prime})\alpha=(1+\varepsilon)\alpha
, as required.

It remains to prove that
b_{1}\leq_{P}\mathsf{first^{\alpha}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(b,P_{1})
and
b_{2}\leq_{P}\mathsf{first^{\alpha}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(b,P_{2})
.
We prove the former, the proof of the latter is similar.
Let
R
be a shortest path from
b
to
w=\mathsf{first^{\alpha}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(b,P_{1})
in
G\setminus\{f\}
(see
Figure

17
.
Let
z
be the first vertex on
R
that is on
P_{1}
and let
y
be the last vertex on
R[b,z]
which is on
P_{2}
.
Let
\beta=\min\{q\in\Gamma\mid q\geq\mathsf{len}(R[b,y])\}
, let
\gamma=\min\{q\in\Gamma\mid q\geq\mathsf{len}(R[y,z])\}
and let
\delta=\min\{q\in\Gamma\mid q\geq\mathsf{len}(R[z,w])\}
.
Notice that
\beta+\gamma+\delta\leq\alpha+3\varepsilon^{\prime}\alpha
.
We will show that
b^{1}_{\beta,\gamma,\delta}\leq_{P}w
, the claim then follows from the minimality (w.r.t
\leq_{P}
) of
b_{1}
on
P
across all choices of
\beta,\gamma,\delta
.

First,
b^{\prime}_{\beta,\gamma,\delta}\leq_{P}\mathsf{first^{\beta}_{G\setminus(P_{1}\cup\{f\})}}(b,P_{2})\leq_{P}y
since
y
can be reached in
G\setminus(P_{1}\cup\{f\})
from
b
with a path (
R[b,y]
) of length at most
\beta
.
Second,
x_{\beta,\gamma,\delta}\leq_{P}\mathsf{first^{\gamma}_{G_{1}}}(b^{\prime}_{\beta,\gamma,\delta},P_{1})\leq_{P}z
since
z
can be reached in
G_{1}
from
b^{\prime}_{\beta,\gamma,\delta}
with a path (
P[b^{\prime}_{\beta,\gamma,\delta},y]\cdot R[y,z]
) of length at most
\gamma
.
Finally,
b^{1}_{\beta,\gamma,\delta}\leq_{P}\mathsf{first^{\delta}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(x_{\beta,\gamma,\delta},P_{1})\leq_{P}w
since
w
can be reached in
G\setminus\{f\}
from
x_{\beta,\gamma,\delta}
with a path (
P[x_{\beta,\gamma,\delta},z]\cdot R[z,w]
) of length at most
\delta
.
∎

6.1
The
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P_{2}\rightarrow P_{1}\rightarrow P}}
labeling

In this section we focus on a vertex
f\in P
.
We denote
G^{\prime}=G^{1}
.
For a vertex
b\in P_{2}
let
D_{b}
be a shortest path from
b
to
\mathsf{first^{\beta}_{G^{\prime}}}(b,P_{1})
. If
\mathsf{first^{\beta}_{G^{\prime}}}(b,P_{1})=null
we say that
b

cannot bypass

f
.
This section is mostly dedicated to proving the following lemma, which leads to a simple labeling scheme for
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P_{2}\rightarrow P_{1}\rightarrow P}}
.

Lemma 6.1
.

There are sequences
I=(b_{1}<_{P_{2}}b_{2}<_{P_{2}}\ldots<_{P_{2}}b_{t})\subseteq P_{2}
and
V=(v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{t})\subseteq P_{1}
such that:

1.

For every vertex
b\in P_{2}
we have
\min_{\leq_{P_{1}}}\{v_{i}\mid b_{i}\geq_{P_{2}}b\}
is an
\mathsf{\varepsilon\beta\text{-}first^{\beta}_{G^{\prime}}}(b,P_{1})
.

2.

Every vertex
b>_{P_{2}}b_{t}
cannot bypass
f
.

3.

t\in O(1/\varepsilon)
.

For
v\in P_{2}
we denote
v_{first}=\mathsf{first^{\beta}_{G^{\prime}}}(v,P_{1})
.
Let
B_{f}
be the set of all pairs
(v,v_{first})
, that are maximal with respect to inclusion (i.e.
B_{f}
does not include a pair
(v,v_{first})
if there exists
(u,u_{first})
with
v<_{P}u
and
u_{first}\leq_{P}v_{first}
).
By the following claim, we can assume that for every
b\in P_{2}
, the path
D_{b}
starts with a prefix
P[b..u]
and procedes with
D_{u}
such that
(u,u_{first})\in B_{f}
.

Claim 6.2
.

Let
b>_{P}f
be a vertex that can bypass
f
.
There is a path from
b
to
b_{first}
beginning with a subpath of
P
followed by some
D_{u}
such that
(u,u_{first})\in B_{f}
.

Proof.

Since
G^{\prime}
does not contain in-going edges into vertices of
P_{2}
(except for the edges of
P_{2}
) and does not contain out-going edges from vertices of
P_{1}
it must be that
D_{b}=P[b,u]\cdot D_{b}[u,b_{first}]
for some vertex
u\geq_{P}b
such that
D_{b}[u,b_{first}]
is internally disjoint from
P
.
Notice that
\mathsf{len}(D_{b}[u,b_{first}])=\mathsf{len}(D_{b})\leq\beta
since
\mathsf{len}(P)=0
, therefore
u_{first}\leq_{P}b_{first}
.
Moreover,
\mathsf{len}(P[b,u]\cdot D_{u})=\mathsf{len}(D_{u})\leq\beta
and therefore, it must be that
u_{first}\geq_{P}b_{first}
, thus
b_{first}=u_{first}
.

Now, let us fix
D_{b}
as a
b
-to-
b_{first}
path of length at most
\beta
that maximizes
u
(with respect to the
\leq_{P}
order).
(Notice that
D_{b}
is not necessarily a shortest path.)
Assume by contradiction that
(u,u_{first})\notin B_{f}
, so there is a vertex
v\in P_{2}
such that
(v,v_{first})\in B_{f}
and
v_{first}\leq_{P}u_{first}
and
u<_{P}v
.
However, in this case
\mathsf{len}(P[b,v]\cdot D_{v})\leq\mathsf{len}(D_{v})\leq\beta
which implies
v_{first}\geq_{P}b_{first}=u_{first}
, and therefore
v_{first}=b_{first}
. The path
P[b,v]\cdot D_{v}
contradicts the maximality of
u
.
∎

Since the endpoints of
P
lie on the same face, a path
D_{b}
that emanates
P
to the right (resp. left) of
P
must enter
P
from the right (resp. left) of
P
.
Let
B_{f}^{left}
and
B_{f}^{right}
be the set of left and right pairs in
B_{f}
, respectively.
Let
G^{\prime}_{right}
(
G^{\prime}_{left}
) be the induced graph from
P
, restricted only to vertices
v
such that there is a path from
P
, that is internally disjoint from
P
, to
v
that emanates
P
to the right (resp. left).

We prove
Lemma

6.1
for the graph
G^{\prime}_{right}
(instead of
G^{\prime}
).
Applying the same argument for
G^{\prime}_{left}
would produce a second pair of sequences
I,V
.
It is straightforward to merge the two
I
’s sequences and the two
V
’s sequences obtained for both cases to complete the proof of
Lemma

6.1
, since any
P_{2}
to
P_{1}
path is contained in either
G^{\prime}_{right}
or
G^{\prime}_{left}
.

An algorithm for
\varepsilon=1
.

We start with a warm-up solution for
\varepsilon=1
(i.e., a 2-approximation).
In this case, we show sequences
I=(b_{1})
and
V=(v_{1})
.
We define
v_{1}
and
b_{1}
as follows.

v_{1}
is the first vertex of
P_{1}
such that there exists a
(b_{v_{1}},v_{1})\in B_{f}^{right}
.

b_{1}
is the last vertex of
P_{2}
such that there is a pair
(b_{1},(b_{1})_{first})\in B_{f}^{right}
.
We show that the sequences
I
and
V
satisfy the lemma for
\varepsilon=1
.
Notice that the demand
t\in O(1/\varepsilon)
is trivial in the current context as
\varepsilon
and
t
are both constants.
Additionally, notice that from the definition of
b_{1}
, every vertex
b>_{P_{2}}b_{1}
cannot bypass
f
.

Claim 6.3
.

If
f<_{P}b\leq_{P}b_{1}
then
v_{1}
is a
\mathsf{\beta\text{-}first^{\beta}_{G^{\prime}}}(b,P_{1})
.

Proof.

Notice that
b_{first}>_{P_{1}}v_{1}
by
Claim

6.2
and the definition of
v_{1}
.
We will show that there is a path of length at most
2\beta
from
b_{1}
to
v_{1}
, which concludes the claim as every
b
can reach
v
via
b_{1}
with a path of length
2\beta
, making
v
a
\mathsf{\beta\text{-}first^{\beta}_{G^{\prime}}}(b,P_{1})
vertex.

Consider the paths
D_{b_{1}}
and
D_{b_{v_{1}}}
.
Since both paths emanate
P
to the right, and since
v_{1}\leq_{P}(b_{1})_{first}<_{P}b_{v_{1}}\leq_{P}b_{1}
(due to both pairs being in
B_{f}
), it must be the case that
D_{b_{1}}
intersects with
D_{b_{v_{1}}}
, say at vertex
z
.
It follows that the path
D_{b_{1}}[b_{1},z]\cdot D_{b_{v}}[z,v_{1}]
is a path of length at most
2\beta
from
b_{1}
to
v_{1}
, as required.
∎

An algorithm for any
\varepsilon
.

We now extend the above solution from
\varepsilon=1
to any
\varepsilon>0
.
We will partition
B_{f}^{right}
into
O(1/\varepsilon)
subsets.
For ease of presentation let us denote
B=B_{f}^{right}
.

Let
A_{1}=(b_{1},p_{1})
and
A_{2}=(b_{2},p_{2})
with
b_{1}>_{P_{2}}b_{2}
be two different pairs in
B
.
We first note that due to the maximality with respect to inclusion
p_{2}<_{P}p_{1}<_{P}f<_{P}b_{2}<_{P}b_{1}
.
Thus, we say that
A_{1}
and
A_{2}

cross
each other, and denote it by
A_{1}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69446pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}A_{2}
.
If there is a path
S
from
b_{1}
to
p_{2}
that is internally disjoint from
P
and is to the right of
P
(by definition of
G^{\prime}_{right}
) of length at most
(1+\varepsilon)r
then we say that
A_{1}
and
A_{2}

good-cross
each other, and denote it as
A_{1}\smash{\scalebox{0.6}{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{good}}}{{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69446pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}}}$}}A_{2}
. Otherwise, we say that they bad-cross each other and denote it by
A_{1}\smash{\scalebox{0.6}{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{bad}}}{{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69446pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}}}$}}A_{2}
.

Lemma 6.4
.

B
can be partitioned into
O(1/\varepsilon)
subsets
B_{1},B_{2},\dots,B_{t}
such that every two pairs in the same
B_{i}
good-cross each other.

Proof.

We present an algorithm that partitions
B
.
The algorithm runs in phases, creating the subset
B_{i}
in the
i
’th phase.
At the beginning of every phase, we initialize a set
B_{good}=\emptyset
.
During a phase, we examine the pairs of
B
in decreasing
<_{P}
order of their first vertex.
When examining a pair
A
, we check if
A
good-crosses all pairs in
B_{good}
, and if so we add
A
into
B_{good}
and remove
A
from
B
.
At the end of the
i
’th phase, we set
B_{i}=B_{good}
and append
B_{i}
to the partition and if
B=\emptyset
the algorithm terminates.
Clearly
|B_{good}|\geq 1
, so the algorithm halts with a partition of the initial
B
.
It should also be clear from the construction of
B_{good}
at every iteration that two pairs in the same
B_{i}
good-cross each other.

Figure 18:
An illustration for the proof of
Lemma

6.4
.
In the top figure, the green wavy path corresponds to
A_{1}
and the blue dotted path corresponds to
A_{2}
.
The sum of the green and blue paths is bounded by
2\beta
.
Since
A_{1}\smash{\scalebox{0.6}{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{bad}}}{{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69446pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}}}$}}A_{2}
, the highlighted red path from
b_{1}
to
p_{2}
is expensive: its length is at least
(1+\varepsilon)\beta
.
Since the yellow and red paths sum up to
\mathsf{len}(A_{1})+\mathsf{len}(A_{2})
, it follows that the highlighted yellow path from
b_{2}
to
p_{1}
is cheap: its length is at most
(1-\varepsilon)\beta
.

The bottom figure demonstrates the next inductive step.
Now, the cheap
b_{2}
-to-
p_{1}
path from the previous step is shown in solid black.
The dashed path from
b_{3}
to
p_{3}
represents
A_{3}
.
As in the top picture, we decompose
A_{3}
and the cheap path into a highlighted red
b_{2}
-to-
p_{3}
path and a highlighted yellow
b_{3}
-to-
p_{1}
path.
It follows from
A_{1}\smash{\scalebox{0.6}{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{bad}}}{{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69446pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}}}$}}A_{2}
that the red path is of length more than
(1+\varepsilon)\beta
.
Since we started with a cheap
b_{2}
-to-
p_{1}
path, the sum of red and yellow is now
2\beta-\varepsilon\beta
, which leads to the length of yellow being at most
\beta-2\varepsilon\beta
.

To conclude the proof, we show that the number of phases
t\leq 1/\varepsilon+1
.
By definition, for every
i\in[2..t]
there is a pair
A_{i}=(b_{i},p_{i})
in
B_{i}
and a pair
A_{i-1}=(b_{i-1},p_{i-1})
in
B_{i-1}
with
b_{i-1}>_{P_{2}}b_{i}
such that
A_{i-1}\smash{\scalebox{0.6}{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{bad}}}{{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69446pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}}}$}}A_{i}
.
Let
A_{t}
be some pair in
B_{t}
and
A_{t-1},A_{t-2},\ldots,A_{1}
be a sequence of pairs with
A_{i-1}\in B_{i-1}
being an arbitrary pair satisfying the above with respect to
A_{i}\in B_{i}
, starting with
A_{t}
.

We claim that for every
i\in[1..t]
there is a path from
b_{i}
to
p_{1}
in
G^{\prime}_{right}
of length at most
(1-(i-1)\varepsilon)\beta
(see
Figure

18
).
The claim is trivial for
i=1
.
We thus assume the claim holds for
i
, and prove it for
i+1
.
Let
P_{1}
be the path from
b_{i}
to
p_{1}
and let
P_{2}=D_{b_{i+1}}
be the path corresponding to the pair
(b_{i+1},p_{i+1})
.
Since
p_{i+1}\leq_{P}p_{1}<_{P}b_{i+1}<_{P}b_{i}
, and since paths only emanate to the right of
P
in
G^{\prime}_{right}
, we must have that
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
intersect at some vertex
z
.
Since
A_{i}\smash{\scalebox{0.6}{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{bad}}}{{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69446pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}}}$}}A_{i+1}
, any path from
b_{i}
to
p_{i+1}
in
G^{\prime}_{right}
is of length more than
(1+\varepsilon)\beta
.
In particular the path
S=P_{1}[b_{i},z]\cdot P_{2}[z,p_{i+1}]
is of length more than
(1+\varepsilon)\beta
.
Let
S^{\prime}=P_{2}[b_{i+1},z]\cdot P_{1}[z,p_{1}]
.
We show that
\mathsf{len}(S^{\prime})\leq(1-i\varepsilon)\beta
.
By the induction hypothesis,
\mathsf{len}(P_{1})\leq(1-(i-1)\varepsilon)\beta
.
By the definition of
B
we have
\mathsf{len}(P_{2})\leq\beta
.
Notice that

\mathsf{len}(P_{1})+\mathsf{len}(P_{2})=\mathsf{len}(S)+\mathsf{len}(S^{\prime})
.
Since
\mathsf{len}(S)>(1+\varepsilon)\beta
we have
\mathsf{len}(S^{\prime})\leq\beta+(1-(i-1)\varepsilon)\beta-(1+\varepsilon)\beta=(1-i\varepsilon)\beta
, as required.

To conclude, if
t>1/\varepsilon+1
then there is a path from
b_{t}
to
p_{1}
of negative length, a contradiction.
∎

We proceed to define the sequences
I
and
V
.
Let
B_{1},B_{2},\ldots B_{t}
be the partition obtained by
Lemma

6.4
.
For every
i\in[t]
, let
p^{\prime}_{i}
be the first vertex of
P
such that there exists a pair
(\cdot,p^{\prime}_{i})\in B_{i}
.
Let
b^{\prime}_{i}
be the last vertex of
P
such that there is a
(b^{\prime}_{i},\cdot)\in B_{i}
.
Assume that the
B_{i}
’s are indexed such that
b^{\prime}_{1}<_{P}b^{\prime}_{2}<_{P}\ldots<_{P}b^{\prime}_{t}

9
9

9

The algorithm described in the proof of
Lemma

6.4
would output
B_{i}
’s exactly in the reverse of this order
.
We define
I=(f,b^{\prime}_{1},b^{\prime}_{2}\ldots,b^{\prime}_{t})
and
V=(p^{\prime}_{1},p^{\prime}_{2},\ldots p^{\prime}_{t})
.

It follows immediately from
Lemma

6.4
that the lengths of the sequences are
O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon})
.
Additionally, note that each
b^{\prime}_{i}
is a last vertex on
P
in its respective
B_{i}
, and
b^{\prime}_{t}
is the last vertex on
P
among all
b^{\prime}_{i}
’s.
Since the sets
B_{i}
’s form a partition of
B
, it holds that
b^{\prime}_{t}
is the last vertex of
P
such that there is a pair
(b^{\prime}_{t},\cdot)
in
P
.
From the definition of
B
, it means that every
b>_{P_{2}}b^{\prime}_{t}
cannot bypass
f
.
We prove via the following claim that
I
and
V
satisfy the remaining property of
Lemma

6.1
.

Claim 6.5
.

For every vertex
b\in P_{2}
we have that
\min_{\leq_{P_{1}}}\{v^{\prime}_{i}\mid b^{\prime}_{i}\geq_{P_{2}}b\}
is a
\mathsf{\varepsilon\beta\text{-}first^{\beta}_{G^{\prime}}}(b,P_{1})

Proof.

First, we claim that for every
b^{\prime}_{i}\geq_{P_{2}}b
, we have
\mathsf{dist}_{G^{\prime}}(b,v^{\prime}_{i})\leq(1+\varepsilon)\beta
.
Recall that
b^{\prime}_{i}
and
v^{\prime}_{i}
are obtained as components of two pairs
(b^{\prime}_{i},\cdot)
and
(\cdot,v^{\prime}_{i})
in
B_{i}
.
Since both pairs are in
B_{i}
, they good-cross each other.
Therefore,
\mathsf{dist}_{G^{\prime}}(b^{\prime}_{i},v^{\prime}_{i})\leq(1+\varepsilon)\beta
via some shortest path
D_{i}
.
We thus have that
P[b,b^{\prime}_{i}]\cdot D_{i}
is a path from
b
to
v^{\prime}_{i}
of length at most
(1+\varepsilon)\beta
.

By
Claim

6.2
there is a pair
(u,b_{first})\in B
such that the path
P[b,u]\cdot D_{u}
is of length at most
\beta
.
Let
B_{j}
be the set that contains the pair
(u,b_{first})
.
Recall that
(b_{j},\cdot)\in B_{j}
is the pair with maximal vertex
b_{j}
(with respect to
\leq_{P_{2}}
) in
B_{j}
, and
(\cdot,v_{j})
is the pair with minimal (with respect to
\leq_{P_{1}}
) vertex
v_{j}
in
B_{j}
.
It follows that
b_{j}\geq_{P_{2}}u\geq_{P_{2}}b
and
v_{j}\leq_{P_{1}}b_{first}
.
We have therefore shown that there is
b_{j}\in I
such that
b_{j}\geq_{P_{2}}b
and
v_{j}\leq_{P_{1}}b_{first}
.
Therefore, in particular, the vertex
b_{i}\geq_{P_{2}}b\in I
that minimizes
v_{i}
also has
v_{i}\leq_{P_{1}}b_{first}
.

In conclusion, for
v=\min_{\leq_{P_{1}}}\{v_{i}\mid b_{i}\geq_{P_{2}}b\}
, we have shown that
\mathsf{dist}_{G^{\prime}}(b,v)\leq(1+\varepsilon)\beta
, and that
v\leq_{P_{1}}b_{first}
.
Therefore,
v
is an
\mathsf{\varepsilon\beta\text{-}first^{\beta}_{G^{\prime}}}(b,P_{1})
as required.
∎

This concludes the proof of
Lemma

6.1
, thus implying a simple labeling scheme for
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P_{2}\rightarrow P_{1}\rightarrow P}}_{\beta,\gamma}
:

Lemma 6.6
.

There exists a labeling scheme
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P_{2}\rightarrow P_{1}\rightarrow P}}_{\beta,\gamma}
with labels of size
\tilde{O}(\mathsf{poly}(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
.

Proof.

Every vertex
u\in P
stores its index in
P
. Moreover,
u
also stores the sequences
I_{u}
and
V_{u}
as defined in
Lemma

6.1
.
Additionally, for every
v\in V_{u}
the label of
u
stores
\mathsf{first^{\gamma}_{G\setminus\{u\}}}(v,P_{1})
and
\mathsf{first^{\gamma}_{G\setminus\{u\}}}(v,P_{2})
.

Lemma

6.1
implies that the length of a label is
\tilde{O}(\mathsf{poly}(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
.

When two labels
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P_{2}\rightarrow P_{1}\rightarrow P}}_{\beta}(b)
and
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P_{2}\rightarrow P_{1}\rightarrow P}}_{\beta}(f)
are given, one can compute
x=\min_{\leq_{P_{1}}}\{v_{i}\mid b_{i}\geq_{P_{2}}b\}
using the label of
f
and the index of
b
, and by the first property of
Lemma

6.1
it is guaranteed that
x
is an
\mathsf{\varepsilon\beta\text{-}first^{\beta}_{G^{\prime}}}(b,P_{1})
, and returns
\mathsf{first^{\gamma}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(x,P_{1})
and
\mathsf{first^{\gamma}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(x,P_{2})
, that were stored explicitly, as required.
∎

6.2
The
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P_{2}\rightarrow P_{2}}}
labeling

In this section, we provide a labeling scheme for
P
such that given the labels of two vertices
f<_{P}b
one can compute
\mathsf{\varepsilon\beta\text{-}first^{\beta}_{G\setminus(P_{1}\cup\{f\})}}(b,P_{2})
.

A labeling scheme for
\varepsilon=1
.

We start with a warm-up solution for
\varepsilon=1
(i.e., when the goal is to compute
\mathsf{\beta\text{-}first^{\beta}_{G\setminus(P_{1}\cup\{f\})}}(b,P_{2})
).

For any vertex
v\in P
, let
v_{last}
be the last vertex in
P
that has a path to
v
in
G
of length at most
\beta
that does not touch any vertex of
P
before
v
.
The pair
(v_{last},v)
is called a
detour
.
Let
D
be the set of all detours
(v_{last},v)
for
v\in P
.
Recall that the
size
of the detour
(v_{last},v)
is the number of vertices in
P[v,v_{last}]
.
We partition the set of detours
D
into exponential classes of sizes, as follows:
For any
i\in[0,\left\lceil{\log_{1.1}|P|+1}\right\rceil]
let
D_{i}
be the set of all detours whose size is in the range
[1.1^{i},1.1^{i+1})
.
Let
x=1.1^{i}
.
We further partition every
D_{i}
into subsets:
For any
k
, let
D_{i,k}
be the subset of
D_{i}
that contains all the detours
(v_{last},v)
where both
v
and
v_{last}
are in the subpath
W_{i,k}=P[0.1\cdot k\cdot x,0.1\cdot k\cdot x+1.2x]
of
P
(which we call a
window
).
An important property is that any vertex
v
of
P
is contained in
O(1)
windows for a specific
D_{i}
and in
O(\log n)
windows in total.

Claim 6.7

(Properties of the subsets)
.

•

Any vertex
v
is contained in
O(\log n)
windows.

•

Let
m_{i,k}=W_{i,k}[\left\lceil{|W|/2}\right\rceil]
be the middle of
W_{i,k}
.
For any detour
(v_{last},v)\in D_{i,k}
we have
v<_{P}m_{i,k}<_{P}v_{last}
.

Let
H_{i,k}
be the (unweighted) graph that is composed of
W_{i,k}
enriched with an edge
(v_{last},v)
for every detour
(v_{last},v)\in D_{i,k}
.
For two detours
(u,v)
and
(x,y)
with
u>x
we say that the detours cross each other, and denote
(u,v)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69446pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}(x,y)
if
u>_{P}x>_{P}v>_{P}y
.
The following claim is used here only for
H=H_{i,k}
, we prove a stronger claim, regarding subgraphs of
H_{i,k}
, which will be useful later.

Claim 6.8
.

Let
H
be a subgraph of
H_{i,k}
.
If there is a
u
-to-
v
path
S
in
H
that does not touch any vertex of
P
before
v
, then there is a
u
-to-
v
path in
H
that does not touch any vertex of
P
before
v
and uses either one or two detours. If it uses two detours, then those detours must cross.

Proof.

Let
e_{1}=(b_{last},b)
be a detour with the latest
b_{last}
in
S
, and let
e_{2}=(a_{last},v)
be the last detour used by
S
.
If
b=v
then the path that goes from
u
to
b_{last}
on
P
and then on
e_{1}
concludes the proof.
Similarly, if
b_{last}=a_{last}
then the path that goes from
u
to
a_{last}
and uses
e_{2}
concludes the proof.
Otherwise, we have
v<_{P}b
and
a_{last}<_{P}b_{last}
.
By
Claim

6.7
we have
b<_{P}m_{i,k}
and
m_{i,k}<_{P}a_{last}
.
Therefore,
v<_{P}b<_{P}a_{last}<_{P}b_{last}
and so the path that goes from
u
to
b_{last}
, on
P
uses
e_{1}
, then goes from
b
to
a_{last}
on
P
, and then uses
e_{2}
contains exactly two crossing detours.
∎

The following claim is a direct consequence of
Claim

6.8
.

Claim 6.9
.

Let
v<_{P}u
be two vertices in
W_{i,k}
.
Then, if
v
is reachable from
u
in
H_{i,k}
with a path that does not touch any vertex before
v
then there is a path in
G
from
u
to
v
that does not touch any vertex before
v
and is of length at most
2\beta
.

Proof.

By
Claim

6.8
there is a path
S
from
u
to
v
that uses at most two detours of
H_{i,k}
.
By definition, each such detour corresponds to a path of length at most
\beta
in
G
.
Moreover, since the total length of
P
is
0
, we have that by replacing each edge in
S
with its corresponding path in
G
, we get a path in
G
of length at most
2\beta
that does not touch any vertex in
P
before
v
.
∎

Using
Claim

6.9
, we can use the auxiliary reachability procedure of Section
4.4
for every set
D_{i,k}
in order to obtain our
2
-approximation:

Every
v\in P
for every
i,k
such that
v\in W_{i,k}
, stores its reachability label in
H_{i,k}
, as well as the minimum vertex
a
such that there is a detour
(a_{last},a)\in D_{i,k}
with
a\leq_{P}v\leq_{P}a_{last}
, if exists.

Recall that by
Claim

6.7
every vertex of
P
is contained in
O(\log n)
windows so the size of
v
’s labels is
\tilde{O}(1)
.

We now explain how to use the information stored in the labels to complete our task.
Let
f
and
b
be two vertices with
f<_{P}b
, and let
p=\mathsf{first^{\beta}_{G\setminus(P_{1}\cup\{f\})}}(b,P_{2})
.
Consider the detour
(p_{last},p)
.
This detour appears in some set
D_{i,k}

(if
(p_{last},p)
appears in more than one
D_{i,k}
, consider one arbitrary such set).
It is clear that
p\leq_{P}b\leq_{P}p_{last}
and therefore
b\in W_{i,k}
.
If
f
is not in this window, then the label of
b
stores
p
explicitly as the minimum vertex that can be reached by a detour starting after
b
.
Otherwise, if
f
is in the window, then we have the labels of the auxiliary reachability procedure of Section
4.4
for both
f
and
b
, which means we can find the first
p^{\prime}>_{P}f
that is reachable from
b
in
H_{i,k}
.
Notice that
p^{\prime}\leq_{P}p
since
(p_{last},p)\in D_{i,k}
implies that
p
is reachable from
b
in
H_{i,k}
.
It follows from
Claim

6.9
that
p^{\prime}
is a
\mathsf{\beta\text{-}first^{\beta}_{G\setminus(P_{1}\cup\{f\})}}(b,P_{2})
.

A labeling scheme for any
\varepsilon
.

We are now ready to solve the problem for any
\varepsilon
.
Let
A_{1}=(u_{1},v_{1})
and
A_{2}=(u_{2},v_{2})
with
u_{1}>u_{2}
be two detours in
D
.
If
A_{1}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69446pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}A_{2}
, and every path from
u_{1}
to
v_{2}
that does not touch any vertex before
v_{2}
is of length more than
(1+\varepsilon)\beta
, then we say that
A_{1}
bad-crosses
A_{2}
and denote
A_{1}\smash{\scalebox{0.6}{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{bad}}}{{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69446pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}}}$}}A_{2}
.
Otherwise, we say that
A_{1}
good-crosses
A_{2}
and denote
A_{1}\smash{\scalebox{0.6}{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{good}}}{{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69446pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}}}$}}A_{2}
.
Notice that if
A_{1}
does not cross
A_{2}
, then
A_{1}\smash{\scalebox{0.6}{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{good}}}{{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69446pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}}}$}}A_{2}
.

From now on, we focus on a single
D_{i,k}
, and use the notation
D=D_{i,k}
,
m=m_{i,k}
,
W=W_{i,k}
and
H=H_{i,k}
.

Lemma 6.10
.

D
can be partitioned into
t=O(1/\varepsilon^{4})
subsets
D_{1},D_{2},\dots,D_{t}
such that if
A_{1},A_{2}\in D_{i}
then
A_{1}\smash{\scalebox{0.6}{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{good}}}{{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69446pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}}}$}}A_{2}
.

Proof.

We present an algorithm that partitions
D
(this is exactly the same algorithm as in the proof of
Lemma

6.4
, but the definition of a bad-cross in this context is different).
The algorithm runs in phases, creating the subset
D_{i}
in the
i
’th phase.
At the beginning of every phase, we initialize a set
D_{good}=\emptyset
.
During a phase, we examine the detours
(u,v)
of
D
in decreasing order of their first coordinate
u
.
When examining a detour
A
, we check if
A
good-crosses all detours in
D_{good}
, and if so we add
A
into
D_{good}
and remove
A
from
D
.
At the end of the
i
’th phase, we set
D_{i}=D_{good}
and append
D_{i}
to the partition and if
D=\emptyset
the algorithm terminates.
Clearly
|D_{good}|\geq 1
, so the algorithm halts with a partition of the initial
D
.

By definition, for a detour
A_{t}=(u_{t},v_{t})
in
D_{t}
, since
A_{t}\notin D_{t-1}
, there is a detour
A_{t-1}=(u_{t-1},v_{t-1})\in D_{t-1}
with
u_{t-1}>u_{t}
and
A_{t-1}\smash{\scalebox{0.6}{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{bad}}}{{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69446pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}}}$}}A_{t}
.
Given
A_{i+1}
(with
i\geq 1
) we define
A_{i}=(u_{i},v_{i})
in a similar way.
Thus, we have a sequence
A_{1},A_{2},\dots,A_{t}
of detours such that
A_{i}\in D_{i}
and
A_{i}\smash{\scalebox{0.6}{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{bad}}}{{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69446pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}}}$}}A_{i+1}
.

Figure 19:
An illustration of the proof of
Claim

6.11
.
The green dashed arrow represents a path of length at most
(1+\varepsilon)\beta
from
u_{i}
to
v_{j}
that does not use a vertex before
v_{j}
on
P
.
Such a path exists due to the assumption that
A_{i}\smash{\scalebox{0.6}{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{good}}}{{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69446pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}}}$}}A_{j}
.
By starting with
P[u_{j-1},u_{i}]
(displayed as a thick green portion of
P
), we obtain a path of the same length from
u_{j-1}
to
v_{j}
that does not use any vertex before
v_{j}
, a contradiction to
A_{j}\smash{\scalebox{0.6}{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{bad}}}{{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69446pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}}}$}}A_{j-1}
.

Claim 6.11
.

For any
1\leq i<j\leq t
we have
A_{i}\smash{\scalebox{0.6}{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{bad}}}{{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69446pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}}}$}}A_{j}
.

Proof.

By
Claim

6.7
,
m
is a position such that for any detour
(u,v)\in D
we have
u>_{P}m>_{P}v
.
Recall that
A_{k-1}\smash{\scalebox{0.6}{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{bad}}}{{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69446pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}}}$}}A_{k}
means in particular that
A_{k-1}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69446pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}A_{k}
thus,
u_{k-1}>_{P}u_{k}>_{P}m>_{P}v_{k-1}>_{P}v_{k}
.
By simple induction
u_{1}>_{P}u_{2}>_{P}\ldots>_{P}u_{t}>_{P}m>_{P}v_{1}>_{P}v_{2}>_{P}\ldots>_{P}v_{t}
.
Thus,
A_{i}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69446pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}A_{j}
.
Assume by contradiction that
A_{i}\smash{\scalebox{0.6}{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{good}}}{{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69446pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}}}$}}A_{j}
, then there is a path
S
from
u_{i}
to
v_{j}
that does not touch any vertex before
v_{j}
and is of length is at most
(1+\varepsilon)\beta
.
The path
S^{\prime}=P[u_{j-1},u_{i}]\cdot S
(see
Figure

19
) is a path from
u_{j-1}
to
v_{j}
that does not use any vertex before
v_{j}
and
\mathsf{len}(S^{\prime})=0+\mathsf{len}(S)\leq(1+\varepsilon)\beta
, a contradiction to
A_{j-1}\smash{\scalebox{0.6}{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{bad}}}{{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69446pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}}}$}}A_{j}
.
∎

Figure 20:
A demonstration of
w_{i},z_{i}
and
S_{i}
.
P_{i}
is black
u_{i}
-to-
v_{i}
path.

For every detour
A_{i}=(u_{i},v_{i})
in the sequence, we define the vertices
w_{i}
and
z_{i}
as follows (see
Figure

20
).
Let
P_{i}
be the path corresponding to the detour
A_{i}
in
G
.
The vertex
z_{i}
is defined to be the first vertex visited by
P_{i}
that is on
P
and has
z_{i}<_{P}m
(notice that
z_{i}
is well defined because
v_{i}<_{P}m
is in
P_{i}
).
The vertex
w_{i}
is the vertex of
P
that precedes
z_{i}
in
P_{i}
(note that
w_{i}
is well defined because
u_{i}>_{P}m>_{P}z_{i}
is the first vertex of
P_{i}
).
Additionally, notice that
w_{i}\geq m
due to the minimality of
z_{i}
in
P_{i}
.
We also define
S_{i}=P_{i}[w_{i},z_{i}]
, and observe that
S_{i}
is internally disjoint from
P
.
If
S_{i}
is to the left (resp. right) of
P
, we say that
(w_{i},z_{i})
is a left (resp. right) pair.
Consider the sequence
W=(w_{1},z_{1}),(w_{2},z_{2}),\ldots,(w_{t},z_{t})
.

By Erdős–Szekeres
[
ES35
]
, there is a subsequence
W^{\prime}
of
W
with
|W^{\prime}|\geq\sqrt{t}
such that the
w_{i}
’s of the pairs in
W^{\prime}
are monotone (either non-increasing or non-decreasing).
By applying Erdős–Szekeres again, we have that there is a subsequence
W^{\prime\prime}
of
W^{\prime}
such that the
z_{i}
values are monotone and
|W^{\prime\prime}|\geq\sqrt[4]{t}
.
Finally, each of the pairs in
W^{\prime\prime}
is either a left or a right pair.
We assume w.l.o.g that most of the pairs in
W^{\prime\prime}
are left pairs.
It follows that there is a subsequence
C
of
W^{\prime\prime}
such that both
w_{i}
and
z_{i}
values are monotone, all pairs in
C
are left pairs, and
|C|\geq|W^{\prime\prime}|/2\geq\frac{\sqrt[4]{t}}{2}
.
To conclude the proof of
Lemma

6.10
, it remains only to prove that
|C|\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon}+1
, since then
\frac{1}{\varepsilon}+1\geq\frac{\sqrt[4]{t}}{2}
.

Claim 6.12
.

|C|\leq 1/\varepsilon+1
.

Proof.

Let
C=(b_{1},c_{1}),(b_{2},c_{2}),\ldots(b_{|C|},c_{|C|})
.
For every
i\in[|C|]
such that
(b_{i},c_{i})=(w_{j},z_{j})
we denote
a_{i}=u_{j}
,
d_{i}=v_{j}
(see
Figure

20
).
From now on, we abuse notation by using
A_{i}
,
P_{i}
and
S_{i}
to refer to
A_{j}
,
P_{j}
and
S_{j}
respectively.

Recall that both
b_{i}
and
c_{i}
are monotone.
We distinguish between two cases:

Case 1: either the
b_{i}
’s or the
c_{i}
’s are non-decreasing.

We assume the
c_{i}
’s are non-decreasing (the proof for the
b_{i}
’s is symmetric).
In this case, we have

a_{1}\leq_{P}a_{2}\leq_{P}\dots\leq_{P}a_{|C|}<m\leq_{P}c_{|C|}\leq_{P}c_{|C|-1}\leq_{P}\dots\leq_{P}c_{1}\leq_{P}d_{1}\leq_{P}d_{2}\leq_{P}\dots\leq_{P}d_{|C|}
.

We will prove by induction that for every
i\in[|C|]
we have
\mathsf{len}(P_{i}[a_{i},c_{i}])\leq(1-(i-1)\varepsilon)\beta
.
For
i=1
the claim follows since
\mathsf{len}(P_{1}[a_{1},c_{1}])\leq\mathsf{len}(P_{1})\leq\beta
.
We assume the claim holds for
i
, and prove it holds for
i+1
.
Notice that (by
Claim

6.11
) for any
i<|C|
we have that the
A_{i}\smash{\scalebox{0.6}{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{bad}}}{{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69446pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}}}$}}A_{i+1}
.
This means that the length of any path from
a_{i}
to
d_{i+1}
that does not use any vertex of
P
before
d_{i+1}
is more than
(1+\varepsilon)\beta
.
In particular, for the path
S=P_{i}[a_{i},c_{i}]\cdot P[c_{i},c_{i+1}]\cdot P_{i+1}[c_{i+1},d_{i+1}]
we have
\mathsf{len}(S)>(1+\varepsilon)\beta
.
Due to the induction hypothesis,
\mathsf{len}(P_{i}[a_{i},c_{i}])\leq(1-(i-1)\varepsilon)\beta
.
Therefore, we have
(1-(i-1)\varepsilon)\beta+0+\mathsf{len}(P_{i+1}[c_{i+1},d_{i+1}])\geq(1+\varepsilon)\beta
which leads to
\mathsf{len}(P_{i+1}[c_{i+1},d_{i+1}])\geq i\varepsilon r
.
Finally, we have
\mathsf{len}(P_{i+1}[a_{i+1},c_{i+1}])=\mathsf{len}(P_{i+1})-\mathsf{len}(P_{i+1}[c_{i+1},d_{i+1}])\leq\beta-i\varepsilon\beta=(1-i\varepsilon)\beta
as required.
If
|C|>1/\varepsilon+1
we get that
\mathsf{len}(P_{|C|}[a_{|C|},c_{|C|}])
is negative, a contradiction.

Case 2: both the
b_{i}
’s and the
c_{i}
’s are decreasing.

We make the following claim:
For every
i\in[|C|]
there is a path
E_{i}
from
a_{i}
to
c_{1}
with
\mathsf{len}(E_{i})\leq(1-(i-1)\varepsilon)r
.
Moreover,
b_{i}
is on
E_{i}
and
E_{i}[b_{i},c_{1}]
is internally disjoint from
P
and is to the left of
P
.
The claim holds for
i=1
by setting
E_{1}=P_{1}[a_{1},c_{1}]
.
We assume the claim holds for
i
, and prove it for
i+1
.

Recall that
P_{i+1}
is the path corresponding to the detour
(a_{i+1},d_{i+1})
and
b_{i}>_{P}b_{i+1}\geq_{P}m>_{P}c_{1}>_{P}c_{i+1}
.
Since both
E_{i}[b_{i},c_{1}]
and
S_{i+1}=P_{i+1}[b_{i+1},c_{i+1}]
are internally disjoint from
P
and go to the left of
P
, they must intersect at some vertex
z\notin P
.

Since
A_{i}\smash{\scalebox{0.6}{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{bad}}}{{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69446pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}}}$}}A_{i+1}
, any path from
a_{i}
to
d_{i+1}
that does not use any vertex before
d_{i+1}
is of length more than
(1+\varepsilon)\beta
.
In particular, for the path
S=E_{i}[a_{i},z]\cdot P_{i+1}[z,d_{i+1}]
we have
\mathsf{len}(S)>(1+\varepsilon)\beta
.
Let
S^{\prime}=P_{i+1}[a_{i+1},z]\cdot E_{i}[z,c_{1}]
. Notice that
S^{\prime}
is a path from
a_{i+1}
to
c_{1}
that goes through
b_{i+1}
and
S^{\prime}[b_{i+1},c_{1}]
, is internally disjoint from
P
, and is to the left of
P
.
It remains to show that
\mathsf{len}(S^{\prime})\leq(1-i\varepsilon)\beta
.
By the induction hypothesis,
\mathsf{len}(E_{i})\leq(1-(i-1)\varepsilon)\beta
.
By definition of a detour we have
\mathsf{len}(P_{i+1})\leq\beta
.
Notice that
\mathsf{len}(E_{i})=\mathsf{len}(E_{i}[a_{i},z])+\mathsf{len}(E_{i}[z,c_{1}])
and
\mathsf{len}(P_{i+1})=\mathsf{len}(P_{i+1}[a_{i+1},z])+\mathsf{len}(P_{i+1}[z,d_{i+1}])
.
Therefore
\mathsf{len}(E_{i})+\mathsf{len}(P_{i+1})=\mathsf{len}(S)+\mathsf{len}(S^{\prime})
.
Since
\mathsf{len}(S)>(1+\varepsilon)\beta
we have
\mathsf{len}(S^{\prime})\leq\beta+(1-(i-1)\varepsilon)\beta-(1+\varepsilon)\beta=(1-i\varepsilon)\beta
, as required.
If
|C|>1/\varepsilon+1
, then there is a path from
a_{t}
to
c_{1}
with negative length, a contradiction.
∎

This concludes the proof of
Lemma

6.10

∎

Let
\mathcal{D}=D_{1},D_{2},\dots,D_{t}
be the partition of
D
obtained by
Lemma

6.10
.
For
D_{i}\in\mathcal{D}
, let
H_{D_{i}}
be the graph that contains
W
(the window corresponding to
D
) and all the detours of
{D_{i}}
(each detour
(v_{last},v)\in{D_{i}}
corresponds to an edge from
v_{last}
to
v
in
H_{D_{i}}
).
The following claim is an extention of
Claim

6.9
for general
\varepsilon
.

Claim 6.13
.

Let
D_{i}\in\mathcal{D}
be a set and let
v<u
be two vertices in
W
.
Then, if
v
is reachable from
u
in
H_{D_{i}}
with a path not touching any vertex before
v
, then there is a path in
G
from
u
to
v
that does not touch any vertex before
v
and is of length at most
(1+\varepsilon)\beta
.

Proof.

By
Claim

6.8
there is a path
S
in
H_{D_{i}}
from
u
to
v
that uses either a single detour, or two crossing detours.
In the case where
S
uses a single detour, the corresponding path in
G
is trivially of length at most
\beta
.
In the other case, let
A_{1}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69446pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}A_{2}
be the two detours.
Since
A_{1},A_{2}\in{D_{i}}
, by definition of the partition it must be that
A_{1}\smash{\scalebox{0.6}{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{good}}}{{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69446pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}}}$}}A_{2}
, and therefore there is a path in
G
of length at most
(1+\varepsilon)\beta
from
u_{1}
to
v_{2}=v
where
A_{1}=(u_{1},v_{1})
and
A_{2}=(u_{2},v_{2})
, as required.
∎

Lemma 6.14
.

There exists a labeling scheme
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P_{2}\rightarrow P_{2}}}_{\beta}
with labels of size
\tilde{O}(\mathsf{poly}(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
.

Proof.

We are using the auxiliary reachability procedure of Section
4.4
in our labeling.

For every
D=D_{i,k}
for every subset of the partition
{D_{j}}\in\mathcal{D}
, each vertex in
W_{i,k}
stores the auxiliary reachability label of
v
in
H_{D_{i}}
.

Additionally,
every vertex
v
in
W_{i,k}
stores the minimum vertex
a
such that there is a detour
(a_{last},a)\in D
with
a\leq_{P}v\leq_{P}a_{last}
, if exists.

Recall that by
Claim

6.7
every vertex of
P
is contained in
O(\log n)
windows, and by
Lemma

6.10
every set is partitioned into
O(1/\varepsilon^{4})
subsets, so the accumulated size of labels kept in
v
is
\tilde{O}(\mathsf{poly}(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
.

We now explain how to use the information stored in the labels to complete our task.
Let
f
and
b
be two vertices on
P
with
f<_{P}b
, and let
p=\mathsf{first^{\beta}_{G\setminus(P_{1}\cup\{f\})}}(b,P_{2})
.
Consider the detour
(p_{last},p)
.
This detour appears in some set
D_{i,k}
(
i
and
k
can be determined by the size of
(p_{last},p)
and the index of
p
).
(If
(p_{last},p)
appears in more than one
D_{i,k}
, consider one arbitrary such set.)
Moreover, there exists a subset
D_{j}
of
D_{i,k}
, obtained via
Lemma

6.10
with
(p_{last},p)\in D_{j}
.
It is clear that
p\leq_{P}b\leq_{P}p_{last}
and therefore
b
is in
W_{i,k}
.
If
f\in W_{i,k}
, then the label of
b
stores
p
explicitly as the minimum vertex that can be reached by a detour starting after
b
.
Otherwise, if
f\in W_{i,k}
, then we have the auxiliary reachability labels of both
f
and
b
in
H_{D_{j}}
, which means we can find the first
p^{\prime}>_{P}f
that is reachable from
b
in
H_{D_{i}}
.
Notice that
p^{\prime}\leq_{P}p
since
(p_{last},p)\in D_{j}
implies that
p
is reachable from
b
in
H_{D_{j}}
.
It follows from
Claim

6.13
that
p^{\prime}
is an
\mathsf{\varepsilon\beta\text{-}first^{\beta}_{G\setminus(P_{1}\cup\{f\})}}(b,P_{2})
.
∎

7
The
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P^{\prime}\xrightarrow{f}P}}
Labeling (Proof of
Lemma

5.7
)

In this section we prove
Lemma

5.7
.
The setting in this section is as follows.

G
(for the ease of presentation we use here
G
as the name of the graph) is a graph,
P
and
P^{\prime}
are two
0
-length paths and
\alpha,\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
.
Our goal is to develop a labeling scheme such that
given the labels of a vertex
a
on
P^{\prime}
and a vertex
f
not in
P^{\prime}\cup P
, one can retrieve the index on
P
of some vertex
b\in P
such that
b
is an
\mathsf{\varepsilon\alpha\text{-}first^{\alpha}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(a,P)
.

Similarly to the case of reachability, we shall define a set of canonical paths that facilitate the distribution of information about avoiding failed vertices. The choice of these canonical paths is more elaborate. The canonical paths are no longer disjoint, but an important feature we maintain is that every vertex
f
of
G
lies on a small number of canonical paths, so we can afford to store in
f
’s label information about these paths when
f
fails.
We work with an estimation
\gamma
that is a multiple of
\varepsilon\alpha
, such that
\gamma-\varepsilon\alpha<\mathsf{dist}_{G}(a,b)\leq\gamma
.
Notice that,
\mathsf{first^{\gamma}_{G}}(P,a)\leq_{P}b
since
\gamma\geq\mathsf{dist}_{G}(a,b)
.
We define
L_{\gamma}=\{(u,\mathsf{first^{\gamma}_{G}}(u,P))\mid u\in P^{\prime}\text{ and }\mathsf{dist}_{G}(u,\mathsf{first^{\gamma}_{G}}(u,P))>\gamma-\varepsilon\alpha\}
.

We define a set
C=C_{\gamma}
of canonical paths (which we call
\gamma
-legitimate paths) as follows.

Definition 7.1

(
\gamma
-legitimate set of paths)
.

A set
C
of paths in
G
is a
\gamma
-legitimate set of paths for
L
if it has the following properties:

•

For each
S\in C
we have
\mathsf{len}(S)\leq(1+\varepsilon)\gamma
and
S=S_{1}\cdot S_{2}
such that both
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
are shortest paths.

•

For every
(c,c_{first})\in L
let
u\geq_{P^{\prime}}c
be the first vertex of
P^{\prime}
with a path
(u\rightsquigarrow u^{\prime})\in C
.
If there are several such paths, let
u^{\prime}
be the first (in
P
) among all vertices
u^{\prime}
.
It holds that
u
exists, and
u^{\prime}
is a
\mathsf{\varepsilon\gamma\text{-}first^{\gamma}_{G}}(c,P)
.

•

For every vertex
f
of
G
there are
O(1/\varepsilon)
paths in
C
going through
f
.

We call the paths in
C

canonical
.
For every
c\in P^{\prime}
let
u\geq_{P^{\prime}}c
be the first vertex of
P^{\prime}
with a path
(u\rightsquigarrow u^{\prime})\in C
(if exists).
If there are several such paths, let
u^{\prime}
be the first (in
P
) among all values
u^{\prime}
.
If
u
exists, we say that
(u\rightsquigarrow u^{\prime})
is
the canonical path of
c
.

In
Section

7.1
we prove the following lemma.

Lemma 7.2
.

For every
G,P^{\prime},P,\varepsilon,\gamma,L
there is a
\gamma
-legitimate set of paths.

Denote such a set by
C=C_{\gamma}
.
We partition
P^{\prime}
into maximal contiguous intervals of vertices
c
with
(c,\cdot)\in L_{\gamma}
that have the same canonical path.
We call these intervals the canonical
\gamma
-intervals of
P^{\prime}
and
P
.
For every such interval
I
of vertices
p
with the same canonical path
S_{I}^{\gamma}=(x\rightsquigarrow y)
, we
call
S_{I}^{\gamma}
the canonical path of interval
I
.

In
Section

7.2
, we prove the following.

Lemma 7.3
.

For a graph
G
, two paths
P
and
P^{\prime}
of length
0
, a path
A=(u\rightsquigarrow u^{\prime})
from the last vertex of
P^{\prime}
to
u^{\prime}
which is the first vertex of
P
and numbers
\alpha,\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
such that:

1.

\mathsf{len}(A)\leq(1+\varepsilon)\alpha
.

2.

A=A_{1}\cdot A_{2}
such that
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
are shortest paths in
G
.

There exists a labeling scheme
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{a\rightsquigarrow P}}=\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{a\rightsquigarrow P}}_{G,P^{\prime},A,P,\alpha,\varepsilon}(v)
such that given the labels of two vertices
a\in P^{\prime}
and
f\in A\setminus P^{\prime}
, one can obtain a vertex
b\in P
such that
\mathsf{dist}_{G\setminus\{f\}}(a,b)\leq(1+\varepsilon)\alpha
and
b\leq_{P}\mathsf{first^{\alpha}_{G\setminus A[\ldots f]}}(a,P)
or conclude that
\mathsf{first^{\alpha}_{G\setminus A[\ldots f]}}(a,P)=null
.
The size of each label is
\tilde{O}(\mathsf{poly}(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
.

We are now ready to prove
Lemma

5.7
, which we restate here.
See
5.7

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon^{\prime}=\varepsilon/2
be an approximation factor and let
\Gamma=\{i\varepsilon^{\prime}\alpha\mid i\in[0,\left\lceil{1/\varepsilon^{\prime}}\right\rceil]\}
be the set of multiples of
\varepsilon^{\prime}\alpha
.
For every
\gamma\in\Gamma
let
C_{\gamma}
be a set of
\gamma
-canonical paths, computed with respect to
\varepsilon^{\prime}
(such a set exists due to
Lemma

7.2
).

For every vertex
v\in P^{\prime}
and for every
\gamma\in\Gamma
such that
(v,\cdot)\in L_{\gamma}
the label of
v
stores: The endpoints
(u_{\gamma},u^{\prime}_{\gamma})
of its canonical path
S_{I_{v}}\in C_{\gamma}
. In addition
v
stores
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{a\rightsquigarrow P}}_{G,P^{\prime}[1,u_{\gamma}],S_{I_{v}},P[u^{\prime}_{\gamma},|P|],\alpha,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(v)

For every vertex
v\in G\setminus P^{\prime}
and for every
\gamma\in\Gamma
and for every
S=(u\rightsquigarrow u^{\prime})\in C_{\gamma}
such that
v\in S
the label of
v
stores:
\mathsf{first^{\alpha+2\varepsilon^{\prime}\alpha}_{G\setminus\{v\}}}(u,P)
and
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{a\rightsquigarrow P}}_{G,P^{\prime}[1,u],S,P[u^{\prime},|P|],\alpha,\varepsilon^{\prime}}(v)
.

Size.

By
Definition

7.1
every
v\in G
is on
O(1/\varepsilon^{\prime})
canonical paths.
Moreover, for every canonical path, the size of
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{a\rightsquigarrow P}}(v)
is
\tilde{O}(\mathsf{poly}(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
by
Lemma

7.3
.
Thus, the total size of every
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{P^{\prime}\xrightarrow{f}P}}(v)
is
\tilde{O}(\mathsf{poly}(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
.

Decoding.

Given the labels of
a\in P^{\prime}
and
f\in G\setminus P^{\prime}
one can compute the index on
P
of some vertex
b
such that
b
is an
\mathsf{\varepsilon\alpha\text{-}first^{\alpha}_{G\setminus f}}(a,P)
as follows.
For every
\gamma\in\Gamma
such that
(v,\cdot)\in L_{\gamma}
let
S_{\gamma}=(u_{\gamma},u^{\prime}_{\gamma})
be the canonical path of
a
in
C_{\gamma}
.
If
f\notin S_{\gamma}
(which can be obtained from the label of
f
) then
b_{\gamma}=u^{\prime}_{\gamma}
.
Otherwise, let
b_{\gamma}^{1}
be a vertex on
P
such that
\mathsf{dist}_{G\setminus\{f\}}(a,b_{\gamma}^{1})\leq(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})\alpha
and
b_{\gamma}^{1}\leq_{P}\mathsf{first^{\alpha}_{G\setminus S_{\gamma}[\ldots f]}}(a,P)
or set
b_{\gamma}^{1}=null
if
\mathsf{first^{\alpha}_{G\setminus S_{\gamma}[\ldots f]}}(a,P)=null
, by using
\mathcal{L}^{\mathtt{a\rightsquigarrow P}}
.
Let
b_{\gamma}^{2}
be
\mathsf{first^{\alpha+2\varepsilon^{\prime}\alpha}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(u_{\gamma},P)
(stored in the label of
f
).
Let
b_{\gamma}=\min_{\leq_{P}}\{b^{1}_{\gamma},b^{2}_{\gamma}\}
.
Finally, we return
b=\min_{\leq_{P}}\{b_{\gamma}\mid\gamma\in\Gamma\}
.

Correctness.

First, we show that
\mathsf{dist}_{G\setminus\{f\}}(a,b)\leq(1+2\varepsilon^{\prime})\alpha=(1+\varepsilon)\alpha
.

•

If
b=b_{\gamma}=u^{\prime}_{\gamma}
it must be that
f\notin S_{\gamma}
and therefore by
Definition

7.1
(first property) we have
\mathsf{dist}_{G\setminus\{f\}}(a,u^{\prime}_{\gamma})\leq\mathsf{len}(P[a,u_{\gamma}]\cdot S_{\gamma})\leq(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})\gamma\leq(1+\varepsilon)\alpha
.

•

If
b=b^{1}_{\gamma}
for some
\gamma
, then by
Lemma

7.3
we have
\mathsf{dist}_{G\setminus\{f\}}(a,b^{1}_{\gamma})\leq(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})\alpha\leq(1+\varepsilon)\alpha
.

•

Otherwise, if
b=b^{2}_{\gamma}=\mathsf{first^{(1+2\varepsilon^{\prime})\alpha}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(u_{\gamma},P)
, by definition
\mathsf{dist}_{G\setminus\{f\}}(a,b^{2}_{\gamma})\leq(1+2\varepsilon^{\prime})\alpha=(1+\varepsilon)\alpha
.

It remains to prove that
b\leq_{P}\mathsf{first^{\alpha}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(a,P)
.
Let
b^{\prime}=\mathsf{first^{\alpha}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(a,P)
and let
R
be a shortest path from
a
to
b^{\prime}
in
G\setminus\{f\}
.
Let
\gamma=x\in\Gamma\mid x\geq\mathsf{dist}_{G}(a,b^{\prime})\}
, and let
S_{\gamma}=(u_{\gamma},u^{\prime}_{\gamma})
be the canonical path of
a
in
C_{\gamma}
.
We distinguish between three cases:

•

If
f\notin S_{\gamma}
,
by
Definition

7.1
(second property)
b_{\gamma}=u^{\prime}_{\gamma}\leq_{P}\mathsf{first^{\gamma}_{G}}(a,P)\leq_{P}b^{\prime}
by definition of
\gamma
.

•

If
R\cap S_{\gamma}[u_{\gamma},f]=\emptyset
, then
b^{1}_{\gamma}
is a vertex on
P
such that
b_{\gamma}^{1}\leq_{P}\mathsf{first^{\alpha}_{G\setminus S_{\gamma}[u_{\gamma},f]}}(a,P)\leq_{P}b^{\prime}
since there exists a path (
R
) in
G\setminus S_{\gamma}[u_{\gamma},f]
from
a
to
b^{\prime}
of length at most
\alpha
.

•

Otherwise (if
R\cap S_{\gamma}[u_{\gamma},f)\neq\emptyset
) we have that
b^{2}_{\gamma}\leq_{P}b^{\prime}
by the following claim.

Claim 7.4
.

b^{2}_{\gamma}=\mathsf{first^{\alpha+2\varepsilon^{\prime}\alpha}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(u_{\gamma},P)\leq_{P}\mathsf{first^{\alpha}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(a,P)=b^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Recall that
(a,\mathsf{first^{\gamma}_{G}}(a,P))\in L_{\gamma}
which means that
\mathsf{dist}_{G}(a,\mathsf{first^{\gamma}_{G}}(a,P))>\gamma-\varepsilon\alpha
.
Assume by contradiction that
b^{\prime}<_{P}b^{2}_{\gamma}
.
Let
z
be some vertex in
R\cap S_{\gamma}[u_{\gamma},f)
.
Let
\ell=\mathsf{len}(S_{\gamma}[u_{\gamma},z])
.
It must be that
\mathsf{len}(R[u_{\gamma},z])\leq\ell-2\varepsilon^{\prime}\alpha
since otherwise,
S_{\gamma}[u_{\gamma},z]\cdot R[z,b^{\prime}]
is a path of length at most
\alpha+2\varepsilon^{\prime}\alpha
, which contradicts the minimality of
\mathsf{first^{\alpha+2\varepsilon^{\prime}\alpha}_{G\setminus\{f\}}}(u_{\gamma},P)
on
P
.
Thus,
\mathsf{len}(R[u,z])\leq\ell-2\varepsilon^{\prime}\alpha
.
Now, consider the path
R[a,z]\cdot S_{\gamma}[z,u^{\prime}_{\gamma}]
(in
G
).
This is a path of length at most
(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})\gamma-2\varepsilon^{\prime}\alpha\leq\gamma-\varepsilon^{\prime}\alpha
from
a
to
u^{\prime}_{\gamma}
in
G
.
Therefore,
u^{\prime}_{\gamma}\geq_{P}\mathsf{first^{\gamma}_{G}}(a,P)
.
By
Definition

7.1
(second property) it must be that
u^{\prime}_{\gamma}\leq_{P}\mathsf{first^{\gamma}_{G}}(a,P)
which leads to
u^{\prime}_{\gamma}=\mathsf{first^{\gamma}_{G}}(a,P)
.
We have shown
\mathsf{dist}_{G}(a,\mathsf{first^{\gamma}_{G}}(a,P))<\gamma-\varepsilon\alpha
this contradicts
(a,\mathsf{first^{\gamma}_{G}}(a,P))\in L_{\gamma}
.
∎

In each case we have shown that
b_{\gamma}\leq_{P}b^{\prime}
, and therefore by the minimality of
b
on
P
, we have
b\leq_{P}b^{\prime}
.
∎

7.1
Selecting canonical paths

In this section we solve the following problem.
We are given a weighted directed planar graph
G
with two paths
P^{\prime}
and
P
where the total length of each path is
0
.
In addition, we are given a length bound
\gamma
, and an approximation factor
\varepsilon>0
.
Finally, we are given a set
L\subseteq P^{\prime}\times P
of pairs
(u,u_{first})
where
u\in P^{\prime}
and
u_{first}=\mathsf{first^{\gamma}_{G}}(u,P)
.
Notice that
L
has the following properties:

1.

Uniqueness:
For every
u\in P^{\prime}
, there is at most one pair
(u,u_{first})
in
L
.

2.

Proximity:
Each pair
(u,u_{first})\in L
has
\mathsf{dist}_{G}(u,u_{first})\leq\gamma
.

3.

Monotonicity:
For every two pairs
(u,u_{first}),(v,v_{first})\in L
, we have that if
u<_{P^{\prime}}v
then
u_{first}\leq_{P}v_{first}
.

Our goal in this problem is to find a
\gamma
-legitimate set of canonical paths for
L
as defined in
Definition

7.1
:
See
7.1

We call the paths in
C

canonical
paths.

The rest of this section is dedicated to the proof of
Lemma

7.2
.
See
7.2

For every
(u,u_{first})\in L
let
A_{u}
be a shortest path from
u
to
u_{first}
(notice that
\mathsf{len}(A_{u})\leq\gamma
).
For every
u<_{P^{\prime}}v
if
A_{u}\cap A_{v}\neq\emptyset
we say that
A_{u}
crosses
10
10

10

A more natural terminology would be to say that
A_{u}
intersects
A_{v}
. However, due to a similarity to
Sections

6.1
and
6.2
we prefer to use the same terminology of crossing here.

A_{v}
and denote
A_{u}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69446pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}A_{v}
.
For every
A_{u}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69446pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}A_{v}
(with
u<_{P^{\prime}}v
) let
z_{u,v}
be the first vertex on
A_{v}
which is also on
A_{u}
(see
Figure

21
).
Let
P_{u,v}=A_{v}[v,z_{u,v}]
and
S_{u,v}=A_{u}[z_{u,v},u_{first}]
be the prefix of
A_{v}
until
z_{u,v}
and the suffix of
A_{u}
from
z_{u,v}
, respectively.
Let
A_{u,v}=P_{u,v}\cdot S_{u,v}
.
If
\mathsf{len}(A_{u,v})\leq(1+\varepsilon)\gamma
we say that
A_{u}
good-crosses
A_{v}
and we denote
A_{u}\smash{\scalebox{0.6}{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{good}}}{{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69446pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}}}$}}A_{v}
; otherwise
A_{u}
bad-crosses
A_{v}
, and we denote
A_{u}\smash{\scalebox{0.6}{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{bad}}}{{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69446pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}}}$}}A_{v}
.

Figure 21:

An illustration of the path
A_{u,v}=P_{u,v}\cdot S_{u,v}
.

Algorithm.

Consider the pairs
(u,u_{first})
in
L
to be ordered according to the
<_{P^{\prime}}
order of
u
.
We construct the set
C
using the following algorithm.
The algorithm initializes
C=\emptyset
and a pair
(a,a_{first})
to be the first pair in
L
.
The algorithm repeats the following procedure until the halting condition is met.
The algorithm finds the last pair
(b,b_{first})\in L
such that
A_{a}\smash{\scalebox{0.6}{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{good}}}{{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69446pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}}}$}}A_{b}
.
We distinguish between two cases:
If
a\neq b
, the algorithm inserts
A_{a,b}
to
C
and sets
(a,a_{first})\leftarrow(b,b_{first})
.
Otherwise, if
a=b
, the algorithm inserts
A_{a,b}=A_{a}
to
C
and sets
(a,a_{first})
to be the pair in
L
following
(a,a_{first})
; if there is no such pair, the algorithm halts and returns
C
.

We prove that
C
is indeed a
\gamma
-legitimate set of canonical paths.

Claim 7.5
.

For each
S\in C
we have
\mathsf{len}(S)\leq(1+\varepsilon)\gamma
and
S=S_{1}\cdot S_{2}
such that both
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
are shortest paths.

Proof.

Let
S\in C
, by the algorithm
S=A_{a,b}
for some vertices
a
and
b
such that
A_{a}\smash{\scalebox{0.6}{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{good}}}{{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69446pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}}}$}}A_{b}
.
By definition of

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{good}}}{{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69446pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}}}

we have
\mathsf{len}(S)=\mathsf{len}(A_{a,b})\leq(1+\varepsilon)\gamma
.
Moreover,
S=A_{a,b}=P_{a,b}\cdot S_{a,b}
and both
P_{a,b}
and
S_{a,b}
are shortest paths (as they are subpaths of shortest paths).
∎

Claim 7.6
.

For every
(c,c_{first})\in L
let
u\geq_{P^{\prime}}c
be the first vertex of
P^{\prime}
with a path
(u\rightsquigarrow u^{\prime})\in C
.
If there are several such paths, let
u^{\prime}
be the first (in
P
) among all vertices
u^{\prime}
.
It holds that
u
exists, and
u^{\prime}
is a
\mathsf{\varepsilon\gamma\text{-}first^{\gamma}_{G}}(c,P)
.

Proof.

Let
(w,w_{first})
be the last pair in
L
.
Notice that the algorithm necessarily iterates the last pair, and therefore adds a path starting in
w\geq_{P^{\prime}}c
to
C
.
Therefore, the vertices
u
and
u^{\prime}
exist.
Let
A_{a,b}=(u\rightsquigarrow u^{\prime})
.
In particular
u=b
and
u^{\prime}=a_{first}
.
Notice that
b\geq_{P^{\prime}}c
.
Moreover it must be that
a\leq_{P^{\prime}}c
since otherwise
C
would also contain some other path
A_{x,a}
or
A_{x,a-1}
(where
(a-1,\cdot)\in L
is the pair preceding
(a,a_{first})
in
L
and
x\in P^{\prime}
) contradicting the minimality of
b
.
It follows from the monotonicity of
L
that
u^{\prime}=a_{first}\leq_{P}c_{first}=\mathsf{first^{\gamma}_{G}}(c,P)
.

Consider the path
S^{\prime}=P^{\prime}[c,b]\cdot A_{a,b}
from
c
to
a_{first}
.
The path
S^{\prime}
has length
\mathsf{len}(S^{\prime})=0+\mathsf{len}(A_{a,b})\leq(1+\varepsilon)\gamma
by
Claim

7.5
.
It follows that
a_{first}
is an
\mathsf{\varepsilon\gamma\text{-}first^{\gamma}_{G}}(c,P)
.
∎

It remains to prove the last property.
For a set
C^{\prime}\subseteq\{A_{u}\mid(u,u_{first})\in L\}
and a vertex
f
we say that
C^{\prime}
is an
f
-bad
set if
f
is on every path in
C^{\prime}
, and every two different paths in
C^{\prime}
bad-cross each other.
We prove the following helpful lemma regarding
f
-bad sets.

Lemma 7.7
.

Let
C^{\prime}
be an
f
-bad set for some vertex
f
of
G
.
It must hold that
|C^{\prime}|\leq 1/\varepsilon+1

Proof.

Let
C^{\prime}=A_{a_{1}},A_{a_{2}},\ldots,A_{a_{t}}
be the paths in
C^{\prime}
ordered such that
a_{1}\leq_{P^{\prime}}a_{2}\leq_{P^{\prime}}\ldots\leq_{P^{\prime}}a_{t}=a_{|C^{\prime}|}
.
We claim that for every
i\in[t]
, we have
\mathsf{len}(A_{a_{i}}[f,({a_{i}})_{first}])\leq(1-(i-1)\varepsilon)\gamma
.

For
i=1
, this is true since
f
is on
A_{a_{1}}
and therefore
\mathsf{len}(A_{a_{1}}[f,({a_{1}})_{first}]\leq\mathsf{len}(A_{a_{1}})\leq\gamma
.
We thus assume the claim holds for
i
, and prove it for
i+1
.

Consider the path

S^{*}=A_{a_{i+1}}[a_{i+1},f]\cdot A_{a_{i}}[f,({a_{i}})_{first}]
.
We first claim that
\mathsf{len}(S^{*})\geq\mathsf{len}(A_{a_{i},a_{i+1}})
.
Recall that
z=z_{a_{i},a_{i+1}}
is the first vertex in
A_{a_{i+1}}
that is also on
A_{a_{i}}
.
Notice that
f\geq_{A_{a_{i+1}}}z
.
There are two cases to consider:

Figure 22:

An illustration of the case
f\geq_{A_{a_{i}}}z
where
S^{*}=A_{a_{i},a_{i+1}}
.

•

if
f\geq_{A_{a_{i}}}z
(see
Figure

22
):
Since both
A_{a_{i}}
and
A_{a_{i+1}}
are shortest paths, we can assume that
A_{a_{i+1}}[z,f]=A_{a_{i}}[z,f]
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle A_{a_{i},a_{i+1}}

\displaystyle=A_{a_{i+1}}[a_{i+1},z]\cdot A_{a_{i}}[z,({a_{i}})_{first}]

\displaystyle=A_{a_{i+1}}[a_{i+1},z]\cdot A_{a_{i}}[z,f]\cdot A_{a_{i}}[f,({a_{i}})_{first}]

\displaystyle=A_{a_{i+1}}[a_{i+1},z]\cdot A_{a_{i+1}}[z,f]\cdot A_{a_{i}}[f,({a_{i}})_{first}]

\displaystyle=A_{a_{i+1}}[a_{i+1},f]\cdot A_{a_{i}}[f,({a_{i}})_{first}]=S^{*}

Hence,
\mathsf{len}(S^{*})=\mathsf{len}(A_{a_{i},a_{i+1}})
.

Figure 23:

An illustration of the case
f\leq_{A_{a_{i}}}z
.

•

if
f\leq_{A_{a_{i}}}z
(see
Figure

23
):
In this case, there exists two subpaths,
A_{a_{i}}[f,z]
and
A_{a_{i+1}}[z,f]

So, we have

\displaystyle S^{*}

\displaystyle=A_{a_{i+1}}[a_{i+1},f]\cdot A_{a_{i}}[f,({a_{i}})_{first}]

\displaystyle=A_{a_{i+1}}[a_{i+1},z]\cdot A_{a_{i+1}}[z,f]\cdot A_{a_{i}}[f,z]\cdot A_{a_{i}}[z,({a_{i}})_{first}]

\displaystyle\text{and \quad}A_{a_{i},a_{i+1}}

\displaystyle=A_{a_{i+1}}[a_{i+1},z]\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\cdot\quad\quad\quad\quad\,\,\,A_{a_{i}}[z,({a_{i}})_{first}]

Therefore,
\mathsf{len}(S^{*})=\mathsf{len}(A_{a_{i},a_{i+1}})+\mathsf{len}(A_{a_{i+1}}[z,f]\cdot A_{a_{i}}[f,z])\geq\mathsf{len}(A_{a_{i},a_{i+1}})
.

Since
A_{a_{i}}
and
A_{a_{i+1}}
are two different paths in
C^{\prime}
, it must be that
A_{a_{i}}\smash{\scalebox{0.6}{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{bad}}}{{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69446pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}}}$}}A_{a_{i+1}}
which means
\mathsf{len}(A_{a_{i},a_{i+1}})>(1+\varepsilon)\gamma
.
Therefore,
\mathsf{len}(S^{*})>(1+\varepsilon)\gamma
.

By definition,
\gamma\geq\mathsf{len}(A_{a_{i+1}})=\mathsf{len}(A_{a_{i+1}}[a_{i+1},f])+\mathsf{len}(A_{a_{i+1}}[f,({a_{i+1}})_{first}])
, which implies

\mathsf{len}(A_{a_{i+1}}[f,({a_{i+1}})_{first}])\leq\gamma-\mathsf{len}(A_{a_{i+1}}[a_{i+1},f])

(1)

By the induction hypothesis
\mathsf{len}(A_{a_{i}}[f,({a_{i}})_{first}])\leq(1-(i-1)\varepsilon)\gamma
which implies

0\leq(1-(i-1)\varepsilon)\gamma-\mathsf{len}(A_{a_{i}}[f,({a_{i}})_{first}])

(2)

By summing
Equations

1
and
2
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathsf{len}(A_{a_{i+1}}[f,({a_{i+1}})_{first}])

\displaystyle\leq\gamma+(1-(i-1)\varepsilon)\gamma-\mathsf{len}(A_{a_{i+1}}[a_{i+1},f])-\mathsf{len}(A_{a_{i}}[f,({a_{i}})_{first}])

\displaystyle\leq(2-(i-1)\varepsilon)\gamma-\mathsf{len}(S^{*})

\displaystyle<(2-(i-1)\varepsilon)\gamma-(1+\varepsilon)\gamma=(1-i\varepsilon)\gamma

as required.

Assume by contradiction that
t=|C^{\prime}|>1/\varepsilon+1
, and deduce that
A_{a_{t}}[f,({a_{t}})_{first}]
is a path of negative length, in contradiction.
Thus,
|C^{\prime}|\leq 1/\varepsilon+1
as required.
∎

Equipped with
Lemma

7.7
, we are finally ready to prove the last property.

Lemma 7.8
.

For every vertex
f
of
G
there are
O(1/\varepsilon)
canonical paths going through
f
.

Proof.

Let
f
be a vertex of
G
and let
C_{f}=\{A_{a,b}\in C\mid f\text{ is on }A_{a,b}\}
.
Recall that for every
A_{a,b}\in C_{f}
,
A_{a,b}=P_{a,b}\cdot S_{a,b}
.
We define two subsets
C_{f}^{P}=\{A_{a,b}\in C_{f}\mid f\in P_{a,b}\}
and
C_{f}^{S}=\{A_{a,b}\in C_{f}\mid f\in S_{a,b}\}
.
Note that
C_{f}^{P}\cup C_{f}^{S}=C_{f}
.

We make the following claim.

Claim 7.9
.

|C_{f}^{S}|\leq 2/\varepsilon+2
.

Proof.

We partition
C^{S}_{f}
into two sets
C_{E}
and
C_{O}
.

C_{E}
and
C_{O}
contain canonical paths of
C^{S}_{f}
that were added to
C
in even and odd steps of the algorithm, respectively.
We proceed by proving that
C^{\prime}_{E}=\{A_{u}\mid A_{u,v}\in C_{E}\}
is
f
-bad, which implies that
|C_{E}|=|C^{\prime}_{E}|\leq 1/\varepsilon+1
via
Lemma

7.7
.
The size of
C_{O}
can be bounded using identical arguments, which yields the claim.

Let
C_{E}=A_{a_{1},b_{1}},A_{a_{2},b_{2}},\ldots,A_{a_{t},b_{t}}
be the canonical paths of
C_{E}
ordered such that
a_{1}\leq_{P^{\prime}}a_{2}\leq_{P^{\prime}}\ldots\leq_{P^{\prime}}a_{t}
.
Clearly, all the paths in
C^{\prime}_{E}
intersect in
f
, so we just need to show that every pair of different path forms a bad-cross.
We start by observing that if the algorithm adds
A_{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}}
to
C
, and two iterations later adds
A_{a^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime\prime}}
to
C
, it must be the case that
a^{\prime\prime}>_{P^{\prime}}b^{\prime}
.
It follows from the monotonicity of the
a
values of
A_{a,b}
added by the algorithm, and by the fact that
C_{E}
contains only paths that were added on an even step of the algorithm that
b_{i}<_{P^{\prime}}a_{j}
for every
i<j\in[t]
.

We claim that for every
i<j\in[t]
, we have
A_{a_{i}}\smash{\scalebox{0.6}{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{bad}}}{{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69446pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}}}$}}A_{a_{j}}
. This follows from
A_{a_{i}}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69446pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}A_{a_{j}}
(due to the intersection of
S_{a_{i}}
and
S_{a_{j}}
in
f
), and from
b_{i}
(
<_{P^{\prime}}a_{j}
) being the last vertex on
P^{\prime}
that participates in a pair of
L
and has
A_{a_{i}}\smash{\scalebox{0.6}{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{good}}}{{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69446pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}}}$}}A_{b_{i}}
.
∎

In addition, we make the following claim.

Claim 7.10
.

|C_{f}^{P}|\leq 3/\varepsilon+3
.

Proof.

The proof is very similar to the proof of
Claim

7.9
, but require a some subtle adjustments.

We partition
C^{P}_{f}
into three sets
C_{0}
,
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
.

C_{0}
,
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
contain canonical paths of
C^{P}_{f}
that were added to
C
in steps numbered with
0
,
1
, and
2
modulo
3
of the algorithm, respectively (i.e.
C_{1}
contains path added in steps
1,4,7,\ldots
).
We proceed by proving that
C^{\prime}_{0}=\{A_{v}\mid A_{u,v}\in C_{0}\}
is
f
-bad, which implies that
|C_{0}|=|C^{\prime}_{0}|\leq 1/\varepsilon+1
via
Lemma

7.7
.
The sizes of
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
can be bounded using identical arguments, which yields the claim.

Let
C_{0}=A_{a_{1},b_{1}},A_{a_{2},b_{2}},\ldots,A_{a_{t},b_{t}}
be the canonical paths of
C_{0}
ordered such that
a_{1}\leq_{P^{\prime}}a_{2}\leq_{P^{\prime}}\ldots\leq_{P^{\prime}}a_{t}
.
Clearly, all the paths in
C^{\prime}_{0}
intersect in
f
, so we just need to show that every pair of different path forms a bad-cross.

We claim that for every
i<j\in[t]
, we have
A_{b_{i}}\smash{\scalebox{0.6}{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{bad}}}{{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69446pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}}}$}}A_{b_{j}}
, by considering two cases.

Case 1:
a_{i}=b_{i}
.

In this case, no pair
(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})
in
L
after
(a_{i},({a_{i}})_{first})
has
A_{a_{i}}\smash{\scalebox{0.6}{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{good}}}{{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69446pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}}}$}}A_{a^{\prime}}
.
In particular,
A_{a_{i}}\smash{\scalebox{0.6}{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{bad}}}{{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69446pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}}}$}}A_{b_{j}}
since
a_{i}<_{P^{\prime}}a_{j}\leq_{P^{\prime}}b_{j}
.
Since
a_{i}=b_{i}
, we have
A_{b_{i}}\smash{\scalebox{0.6}{$\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{bad}}}{{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.33333pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.69446pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.36115pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\mathrel{-}\joinrel\mathrel{-}}}}}}$}}A_{b_{j}}
as required.

Case 2:
a_{i}\neq b_{i}
.

Let
3k
be the iteration number in which
A_{a_{i},b_{i}}
was added to
C
.
In this case, the algorithm will process the pair
(b_{i},({b_{i}})_{first})
in iteration
3k+1
.
In this iteration, the path
A_{b_{i},c}
would be added to
C
such that
(c,c_{first})\in L
is the last pair such that
